## Part opening

<!-- pedagogy-unit: part:OBSERVATIONS::part-opening -->

Part ID: `part:OBSERVATIONS`

Purpose:

> Neural fluctuations reach behavior through a motor pathway, while spatial tuning describes how a position is expressed across cells. The eye and place-field studies constrain these different relationships. The final chapter connects their results without treating a static map as a complete dynamical model.

## ch:EYE-DRIFT - Eye drift points to a shared neural drive

Source file: `content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md`

Purpose: Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called fixational drift. Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them? Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.[^p2-question]

Prerequisites: ch:PERSISTENCE, ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS

Sidecar sections: From stored eye position to muscle action, The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time, A fitted model links movement to firing rate, A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes, The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone, Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance

Blocks: 2 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called fixational drift. Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them? Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.[^p2-question]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 ca0bc9dead0d4e995e3e496d098a0a1213854eccf928570a5bf5ff1473f4f55a -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> This distinction connects the previous chapters to a measurement outside the brain. A memory coordinate can diffuse inside a neural network, but the movement produced by that coordinate also depends on the muscles and the mechanics of the eye. We need to follow the signal through those stages before comparing a neural diffusion law with an eye trajectory.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 3240811d7c0d01b6d80c1c03bc1ac671c9735238e431fb6625d93370d9d8c698 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> A recording from one motor neuron becomes useful for this comparison only after its spikes and the eye's movement can be expressed on the same scale. The larger movements provide that calibration. Applying it during fixation then lets us ask whether independent motor noise could explain the observed relationship, and estimate the shared contribution under explicit gain and independence assumptions.

### From stored eye position to muscle action

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:EYE-PATHWAY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 21

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 ccd80d6128f360a3fe92c364f6d30e0b9ac0a957260418c96f3fd15c30e8a97b -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> The eye needs an ongoing command to maintain a position. A brief command to move is therefore insufficient: after the movement, a circuit must maintain activity representing the new position. The [**oculomotor integrator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the neural circuit that performs this storage. “Integrator” refers to turning a movement command into a lasting position signal. In the proposed mechanism, the continuous attractor described in the [foundations](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-attractor) and [network model](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-spikes) stores horizontal eye position. Noise moves the stored value along that attractor.[^p2-pathway]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 326229916f9e18672b79d20aa5c7df46733a620ecb497ec93a340ebbb62f64d5 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p15

> The signal reaches [**ocular motoneurons (OMNs)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, neurons whose spikes activate the eye muscles. The recordings came from the [**abducens nucleus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a group of brainstem neurons that includes OMNs activating the lateral rectus muscle, which turns the eye outward. Other neurons in that nucleus carry signals to other neurons rather than directly to muscle. The study separated these cell types using their eye movement response parameters. Finally, the muscles and the mechanical properties of the eye, together called the [**ocular plant**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, turn neural activity into movement.[^p2-recordings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 3af281d10f19ea5af76f2268dd00bc99b63c5b0aee7bc65e9eae421bc3f2ab84 -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p17

> There are three possible contributions to an observed drift:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 62ac9efe5322aa0da540d474f94aaa95f5da3a9d44275a070fb362ebeea31c6d -->
p17, lines 17-17; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p18

> A changing shared command sent to many OMNs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p18 sha256 99f5fdcaccef21a06249954e75433916d06015b789624b3bc35cbaf79820bad1 -->
p18, lines 18-18; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p19

> Private spike variability in individual OMNs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 0249dc07c5304816448f61c79df07363b728ff66017cf18997a826c1434b65ee -->
p19, lines 19-19; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p18; next: p21

> Variability after those neurons, including muscle and measurement noise.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 eb36b8b826e9c9f9447146948653c6fe453b360600fe2f094d766db6576fcc29 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p25

> Private spike fluctuations can partly cancel when many neurons act together. A common command survives this averaging. This is why the noisy activity of one neuron can reveal a source that dominates the much smoother eye movement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p25 sha256 f8a740447bda1c1c53c16adebc30af97cc2b73076a1419a10bfee56992b223b1 -->
p25, lines 25-25; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p27

> The authors reanalyzed earlier experiments in two male rhesus macaques. The animals first fixated a stationary target and then tracked a moving target. A wire search coil on one eye measured eye position while an electrode recorded one neuron's spikes. There were 57 identified OMNs. Each recording paired one OMN with eye movement; there were no simultaneous recordings of multiple OMNs. Thus the analysis cannot directly measure the covariance between two recorded motor neurons.[^p2-recordings][^p2-single]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 2619e69fdb09ac2d7d66b5c6495d49fb63dd6924a2b866b628207d75ff7cd44d -->
p27, lines 27-27; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p25; next: p29

> The initial stationary target was followed by 500–700 ms of required fixation before the target stepped and began moving. Neural voltage was sampled at 25 kHz and eye position at 1 kHz. The authors detected microsaccades using eye speed and acceleration, removed 30 ms on each side of them, and manually checked the remaining segments. For the main displacement analysis, segments shorter than 350 ms were excluded and longer ones were split into nonoverlapping 350 ms pieces. There were 302 such pieces per cell on average, with different counts across cells. These repeated observations improve estimates within two animals; they do not create 57 independent animal samples.[^p2-segments]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 a23735ac7bf8726d98cc30c9927e5380c6d6c4120f669b9ff604754b780f72a3 -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p31

> The large movements have a useful role even though the scientific question concerns tiny drifts. They produce enough rate change to estimate how each cell's activity depends on eye movement. The fitted relationship is then applied during fixation. This transfer is an assumption to examine, because an incorrect gain during drift would change the inferred common variance. [**Gain**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the scale that converts a change in one signal into a change in the other.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p31 sha256 2455077ea873544e8751a359f556bad9e2360dcfd3d084fb56fe958abd535cf2 -->
p31, lines 31-31; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p33

> The diagram separates the physical pathway from the paired recordings and the later calculation of predicted eye position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p33 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p33, lines 33-33; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p31; next: p37

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p37, lines 37-37; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p33; next: p39

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p39 sha256 33adabde654e5601e7e317575a762b5ecae78bae70b3b4eccf75adc46aaceb9d -->
p39, lines 39-39; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p37; next: p43

> The upper path describes a possible physical source and its output. The recording design provides one cell and eye movement at a time; it does not measure covariance between simultaneously recorded motor neurons. The predicted position is a later analysis result, not a signal sent backward through the physical pathway.

### A fitted model links movement to firing rate

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p43 sha256 2d5a4bd1401864e33fc9fc4990186090a957c2651f19cbb7b6cbd5e46eb26288 -->
p43, lines 43-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p39; next: p50

> Position alone does not predict a motor neuron's activity during a movement. At the same position, firing can differ when the eye is moving or accelerating. The paper therefore fits

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 35fbef1321d69097d5535fe106bd53ca030f8ada3646621fe938d6269e80e804 -->
p50, lines 50-50; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p43; next: p58

> Here $E$ is horizontal eye position in degrees and $\widehat f$ is predicted firing rate in spikes/s. The position threshold $E_T$ sets where the position term reaches zero. The coefficients have units

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p58 sha256 c429a3f0eb82cb1acf60cb183ba8a898a8e568f9a0da15d3a0bcee8853ffa5c5 -->
p58, lines 58-58; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p60

> Multiplying by position, velocity, and acceleration gives spikes/s in all three terms. The delay $\Delta t$ is in seconds: firing at time $t$ predicts movement at the later time $t+\Delta t$ for a positive neural lead. This is the paper's Eq. 2, including its threshold and time shift.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p60 sha256 f3fc314e12bf31a6fd59e7b34dfb99e911184c925b34e79a5945cd1023947062 -->
p60, lines 60-60; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p58; next: p67

> The observed rate $f(t)$ was estimated as the inverse of the interval between successive spikes, constant between those spikes. For each cell, the authors minimized the sum of squared rate errors,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p67 sha256 c36d17f381179b57d22c89b867c01ec25faa88683b6c1562b24179d0ee188176 -->
p67, lines 67-67; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p60; next: p69

> $a$ labels trials and $T_a$ is trial duration. This is Eq. 3 with the trial count renamed so that it cannot be confused with a neuron count. Fits used full trials, including fixation and pursuit. To predict a trial's rate, that trial was left out of the parameter fit. Acceleration was estimated from velocity after local polynomial smoothing over 21 ms. Trials in which the rate fell below 20 Hz were discarded: the linear formula is intended for active cells above firing threshold, whereas firing cannot become negative.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 e7c421b6dbe3a43a533b79a2e228dd1b59209fd8d1af1c8f68a7562f9624bb7c -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p67; next: p73

> During fixation, the authors subtracted each segment's mean firing rate, joined the segments, and compared observed and predicted rate fluctuations. Figure 1c illustrates a weak relationship for one cell; Fig. 1d shows a positive population effect. The one sided test asks whether the mean correlation is positive, compared with a zero-mean reference. Its [**p value**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the probability, under that reference, of a test statistic at least as large as observed. Across 57 cells it gave $p=3\times10^{-9}$. A weak single cell relationship is expected here because the tiny movement produces small rate changes against much larger spike variability. This is the first evidence of a relationship, before any estimate of the fraction of common motion.[^p2-rate-result]

### A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes

Location: line 71, anchor `sec:EYE-INVERSE-FILTER`

Closing passage line: 161

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p73 sha256 bde35c11ee6ec82d0faafab73d5dce7cf71d5e140a64ea7f6bf8e66ee4f55ace -->
p73, lines 73-73; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p75

> To ask how much eye movement is shared with a neuron, it helps to express both signals in degrees. The paper reverses the fitted relationship: a cell's spike train becomes the input, and its prediction of eye position becomes the output. This operation is an inverse of the movement-to-rate fit, rather than a new fit to small drift residuals.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p75 sha256 4f6c7d464c5f055b961eb1cbfceab1493dc76af31b519d8f4014631f024aa40f -->
p75, lines 75-75; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p73; next: p81

> Let $x(t)=E(t)-E_T$. Ignoring the time shift for a moment, the deterministic relation is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 7f48654e5c8ea2f33c055c5899c633f4f0fe48408a259f6f7406c7737c73172a -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p75; next: p88

> A [**causal filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} computes its present output from present and past input. For the equation above, its [**impulse response**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $h_0(u)$ is the position response to one instantaneous spike at time zero. To factor the equation, consider a time component $e^{pt}$: differentiation multiplies it by $p$. The algebraic ratio of output to input for that component is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p88 sha256 19dbd09a54232a01ba1f66b8e50cd5c1f1d56443af3ae6383d94723a2d4a446f -->
p88, lines 88-88; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p95

> Factoring the quadratic gives the slow and fast decay times

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p95 sha256 efae1bfe1de1d6e6373840e64544427f7b1283ecf19a2cd03beaa0d935fe4881 -->
p95, lines 95-95; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p88; next: p102

> These are the paired signs in the paper's Eq. 7, with $\tau_+>\tau_-$ when $k,r,m>0$ and $r^2>4km$. In this case both components decay without oscillation. The same factorization gives two useful identities:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p102 sha256 a2ac4d749ee4165fb46a31c375d552a55ed758a66b963b8690e4d5da22e3b600 -->
p102, lines 102-102; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p95; next: p110

> The corresponding impulse response is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p110 sha256 17b1baaff251def1ac150a79aebb365830c810003d113f5b384376630eed77b0 -->
p110, lines 110-110; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p102; next: p117

> $\Theta(u)$ is zero for $u<0$ and one for $u>0$; it imposes causality. Restoring the fitted neural lead delays this response: $h(u)=h_0(u-\Delta t)$. Write the spike train as $\xi(t)=\sum_j\delta(t-t_j)$, where $\delta$ is an ideal instantaneous impulse of unit area, so integration over one spike counts one event. The prediction is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p117 sha256 94fdba405561135fb2ffbc1d55ee5e10ed896152ae5250c4fac6439430ea4c04 -->
p117, lines 117-117; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p110; next: p121

> These expressions reproduce Eqs. 4–6, including the factor $\tau_+\tau_-/m$ and the delay. The [**convolution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} integral adds the responses to all past spikes. With spike count treated as a count, $\xi$ has units spikes/s and $h$ has units degrees/spike. Each spike contributes a decaying position response; adding those responses produces degrees.[^p2-inversion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p121 sha256 4eec0db35e0cd5097e595acded14851f798b0df8940c381d3a89cfa31f16d34f -->
p121, lines 121-121; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p117; next: p127

> We can obtain the response to a spike directly from the differential equation. After the spike, the input is zero, and each of the two exponentials solves the remaining homogeneous equation. Write their combination as

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p127 sha256 d5a34ccb8a05bed2a1a5184b92b28398d972bd5b9f8f5e276f2e8d2167d6b11f -->
p127, lines 127-127; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p121; next: p134

> To find $A$ and $B$, treat the spike as a short pulse of unit area and shrink its duration to zero. The position change during that pulse tends to zero, so $h_0(0^+)=0$. Integrating $m h_0''+r h_0'+k h_0$ across the pulse gives $m h_0'(0^+)=1$: the velocity changes by $1/m$, while position and velocity before the causal response are zero. Therefore

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p134 sha256 cf9badc5a588635909f7997060bc4015fc6ee7aaada1d42cb3a7fbf0a4ce583a -->
p134, lines 134-134; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p127; next: p138

> Substituting $B=-A$ gives $A(1/\tau_- -1/\tau_+)=1/m$, hence $A=\tau_+\tau_-/[m(\tau_+-\tau_-)]$. Making the response zero before the spike gives the impulse response $h_0(u)$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p138 sha256 9b2ee830691f3039bde3143aa4ee58dbc946248e991762c153bd2baf666366b1 -->
p138, lines 138-138; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p134; next: p145

> For a constructed example, take $k=4$ spikes/(s deg), $\tau_+=0.120$ s, and $\tau_-=0.005$ s. The identities above require $r=0.500$ spikes/deg and $m=0.00240$ spikes s/deg. Then

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p145 sha256 8c7bd8b13dc15819406aa2a41498f782c06fdd5848a2979e41a963c86d646976 -->
p145, lines 145-145; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p138; next: p149

> and its area is $2.174(0.120-0.005)=0.250$ deg s/spike, equal to $1/k$. A steady rate of 80 spikes/s predicts an offset of 20 degrees above $E_T$. This checks the gain as well as the time constants; it is not a fit to a recorded cell.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 1682b05d154bb130d709606004cc56a1752f384ec76339a5c9586211f2f1c302 -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p157

> The kernel's area checks its normalization:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 9342257b323d29a7490feb94bbe753fbac3b7da45624d5c8c5781848515e0a92 -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p161

> Thus a constant input rate $f_0$ predicts $E_T+f_0/k$, exactly the static forward relation. A kernel with unit area would give the wrong conversion unless $k$ had already been absorbed into the input. When the two decay times coincide, $r^2=4km$, the limit is $h_0(u)=\Theta(u)u e^{-u/\tau}/m$. The distinct exponential formula is used for the fitted responses in the paper.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p161 sha256 2f42fd739920fd78587ec66c674b3c2ec94a21bf618217a41963038e35b868b0 -->
p161, lines 161-161; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p165

> Figure 2a–b shows typical fitted times of about 5 ms and 120 ms. Figure 2c shows that a single cell's reconstructed drift is much noisier than the measured movement. Across 350 ms displacement pairs, the mean Pearson correlation was $\langle R\rangle=0.17\pm0.02$, where the error is the [**standard error of the mean (SEM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. SEM estimates uncertainty in the across-cell mean, rather than the spread of individual cells or uncertainty across animals. The one sided test gave $p=3.8\times10^{-12}$. Shuffling which measured segment was paired with which spike prediction centered the comparison near zero. Here and below $\langle\cdot\rangle$ over cells means a population average.[^p2-correlation]

### The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone

Location: line 163, anchor `sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 165
Closing passage line: 197

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 b54158d1b0264f8858ec0971143edd70bb5633c70c4b329e7ea088dd725c42bf -->
p165, lines 165-165; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p161; next: p167

> A correlation of 0.17 does not immediately tell us how much motion comes from the common command. First we can ask a more limited question: could all the motion come from independent motor neuron fluctuations?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p167 sha256 7b91bf7f7698525cd5c8f1eb6f2895f170f6d4d45c4d9a54f438660a098f924f -->
p167, lines 167-167; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p175

> For a centered displacement $Y$ and one centered prediction $X$, the best linear prediction $bX$ minimizes $\operatorname{Var}(Y-bX)$. Differentiating with respect to $b$ gives $b=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)/\operatorname{Var}(X)$. Its explained fraction of variance is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p175 sha256 5e6c5d292149e1cff11a4989c8f329d9f8f425a66076641ee95848e2644e5f6c -->
p175, lines 175-175; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p177

> This is the [**coefficient of determination**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for a linear prediction with a fitted gain. It does not assert that the raw, unscaled position predictor itself has a small squared error.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p177 sha256 19e045d8607cf3876e73fdbde10520406a5e677e00c0c12c4b2e1857a8d6b503 -->
p177, lines 177-177; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p185

> Suppose there are $N_m$ independent single neuron predictors $X_i$. Standardize each to variance one and also standardize $Y$. Independence makes the $X_i$ uncorrelated, so the residual after using them all has variance

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p185 sha256 87bd59ca3ad47cbbf9f483a691c623d8d48f9f9801046f709ee61ad1b5739a64 -->
p185, lines 185-185; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p177; next: p187

> Consequently $\sum_iR_i^2\le1$, and an average neuron explains at most $1/N_m$. This proves the bound used in Supplementary Eq. S1. It does not require equal muscle effects from all neurons. Applying it to the sampled cells does require their average explanatory power to represent the relevant motor population.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p187 sha256 0514e747eaf71b383c2e16919c381e3a8ef595ae2542aeaa531c338157ab6990 -->
p187, lines 187-187; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p185; next: p195

> The authors avoid directly averaging noisy squared correlations. Instead,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p195 sha256 10e160e7aaec8a9ca157723de606011692baf3f7538c100a731e753b8de2bad2 -->
p195, lines 195-195; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p187; next: p197

> Thus the estimated mean correlation implies an average explained fraction of at least about 2.9%. For 1,000 independently contributing motor neurons, the bound would be 0.1%; for several thousand it is smaller. The mismatch argues against independent OMN noise as the sole source. Shared fluctuations can allow many cells to explain the same part of the movement, so their explained fractions can overlap.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p197 sha256 56567703098d6aaa1d1e145327c006422918468b8966daa0ac8fff9abcc8dcc9 -->
p197, lines 197-197; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p195; next: p201

> This step finds shared variation. Locating its source upstream also uses a biological premise: the private variability generated within OMNs is taken to be independent across cells. The paper supports that premise using anatomical and stimulation studies indicating little local recurrent coupling in the relevant motor nuclei. It was not tested by simultaneous recordings in this data set. Common inputs can correlate the total activity of OMNs even when their privately generated spike noise is independent.[^p2-independence]

> The variance bound establishes that a shared signal exists, but leaves its size open. The next calculation uses the fact that the inverse filter has a calibrated gain in degrees. For one 350 ms interval, write the measured displacement and the single cell prediction as

p210, lines 210-210; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p208; next: p211

p211, lines 211-211; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p210; next: p213

p213, lines 213-213; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p211; next: p222

p229, lines 229-229; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p222; next: p231

> These are Eqs. 11–15 in displacement form. The gain assumption requires that the relation calibrated during larger movements still applies during drift. There is also a small covariance between $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, because the recorded neuron's private spikes contribute both to its predictor and to actual eye motion. The paper neglects this contribution as order $1/N_m$ under its many neuron model.[^p2-chi-method]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p231 sha256 01e7757b7ffe43dfc2273cd2895457e71bb691dd44694c85207a8e7d89aa11eb -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p241 sha256 1dc51e3ecb390dbfdf30bcb72a02357ed259fba0c9a4796682ab9c24ae89de9d -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p258 sha256 7c010ba891d1203aae0b9cdfb828ee1a6349bec617989cae78059d6e471dfcc1 -->
p258, lines 258-258; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p256; next: p260

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p266 sha256 7092966ea3b73970b3202c2bb54e21f73ef4164d966c5c6ccb2b808501e1b297 -->
p266, lines 266-266; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p264; next: p268

> Private prediction noise enlarges the denominator of R without changing the covariance. The bars use the same physical scale, so the equal common pieces have equal lengths. See [Shared and private variability, Eqs. 11–15, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p268 sha256 a65b6fdc44c554175747a64e282a650477b18593ddf8eb1d86a93d5a0618d3a5 -->
p268, lines 268-268; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p266; next: p276

> In the experiment, each cell gives a noisy estimate $\chi_i$, and those estimates can lie outside $[0,1]$. The underlying variance fraction remains in that interval under the model, but the sample covariance need not. The paper estimates each standard error $\Delta\chi_i$ mainly from covariance uncertainty, treating the measured displacement variance in the denominator as comparatively well determined. It then uses inverse variance weighting:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p276 sha256 dce33de73aed51db8944cea730d1c0616c4da4f648d765856356d76fd5cd597b -->
p276, lines 276-276; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p268; next: none

> To see the weighting, let normalized weights $a_i$ sum to one. Independent errors give variance $\sum_i a_i^2(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ for $\sum_i a_i\chi_i$. Minimizing this subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$ gives $a_i\propto1/(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ and the error above. The paper additionally assumes approximately normal errors around a common value. The last expression is a propagated estimation error under those assumptions. It does not measure variation across animals or uncertainty in the gain and independence assumptions. The reported result was $0.82\pm0.07$, a weighted mean and weighted error for horizontal displacement over 350 ms. It is neither a fraction of distance traveled nor the fraction that one neuron's spikes accurately predict.[^p2-chi-error]

## ch:EYE-TIMING - Drift growth distinguishes diffusion from filtered white noise

Source file: `content/chapters/04b-eye-timing.md`

Purpose: The shared movement estimate concerns a single interval length. It leaves open whether the command merely fluctuates around a stable value or keeps accumulating changes. Comparing displacements over different time separations can distinguish these possibilities, but the smallest measured displacements may include a large contribution from recording error.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT

Sidecar sections: Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD, The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion

Blocks: 2 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> The shared movement estimate concerns a single interval length. It leaves open whether the command merely fluctuates around a stable value or keeps accumulating changes. Comparing displacements over different time separations can distinguish these possibilities, but the smallest measured displacements may include a large contribution from recording error.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 67f5d8a6a1a82ef6848a40693e7df754d2e0adcd101947315a06c81f7341df5e -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> The mean squared displacement measures the average squared position change over a chosen interval. Separating the recording error from that quantity is necessary before interpreting its growth. To test whether the command accumulates changes, we compare two possible inputs after a decaying muscle response: fluctuations that do not accumulate and a position that wanders by diffusion. Their different long interval behavior explains how a smooth eye movement can still reflect a wandering stored value.

### Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 49

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 90b024b2aaaf61ea685d07c0fd93c6ee94b4196b59c95b96526816f1ef60d77d -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p15

> The common variance calculation uses one interval length. To learn what kind of process drives motion, the authors next compare displacements at many time separations. Using the [earlier definition of mean squared displacement](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-covariance),

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 61e04c7a4d88eae14f10f9fb9dfbcc2d3fea58e34f6077a8eabf1ac7f5d59b63 -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p17

> is the mean squared displacement, or MSD, at lag $\ell$. The data estimate it within each microsaccade free segment, then average the available segment estimates at each lag. Larger lags have fewer eligible segments. The 1 ms sampling sets the available lag spacing.[^p2-msd]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 152949ab575328d32f2822b44acf9df72b8bacd3d378a2aad5fdc3895894c14c -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p19

> At short lags, the displacement can be smaller than the search coil's measurement error. Suppose its reading is $E_{\rm obs}(t)=E(t)+\nu(t)$. The measurement error is assumed to have:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 5768505d76ac7e939baaff1c40a7a4c6e00acf5ccebf83fb2bba0287f07732ce -->
p19, lines 19-19; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p20

> Zero mean and variance $\sigma_m^2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p20 sha256 0775085bf03157799ee96fd6888b06928f2c2c3db4a0a5eaabc13d8bc5cc4526 -->
p20, lines 20-20; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p21

> Independence from true movement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 2270208b4d7255eab106fe5a80e3d004d88ac293a6f1d3cb81b78b3a41d98760 -->
p21, lines 21-21; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p20; next: p23

> No correlation between distinct sampled times.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 6e62261cd1b9232e0ef2b260b3c11235b8716d400b8d44e2b1069f6b8e836bdd -->
p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p35

> For a positive lag,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 e0d20ca4b4a0a1461ac3cdbe79c18120c7eab1f23e8b468e4f451040785f920f -->
p35, lines 35-35; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p37

> The mixed movement-error term vanishes by independence. This is Eqs. 18–19. The correction is the variance of a difference of two errors, $2\sigma_m^2$, rather than the variance of a single position error. At exactly zero lag the same error is subtracted from itself, so the MSD is zero. A positive lag plateau near zero lag can still occur when successive measurement errors are independent.[^p2-msd-noise]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 a19a889a1b976783f7fe5467fce2658ff9eb64d3b301975383306afb4c0ee1a8 -->
p37, lines 37-37; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p39

> The authors estimated the plateau contribution at order $10^{-3}$ deg$^2$, checked its scale with a coil fixed in space, and subtracted that contribution from the observed MSD. This is a correction of an average statistic, not knowledge of each trial's unknown noise. Smoothing the eye trajectories instead would also change the movement statistics the analysis seeks to measure. If the plateau estimate has uncertainty $10^{-4}$ deg$^2$, a corrected MSD of that size is unreliable even when the plateau itself is much larger. The peer review response uses this example to explain the shortest lag limitation.[^p2-noise-limit]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p39 sha256 3d3ef08daebdef4d2f9c40989a971e5f939a96f451bf400685ca9fc0edd83f3d -->
p39, lines 39-39; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p37; next: p41

> For instance, a constructed measured MSD of $0.0014$ deg$^2$ with an estimated noise contribution $0.0010$ deg$^2$ leaves $0.0004$ deg$^2$. A $0.0001$ deg$^2$ uncertainty in the subtraction is already 25% of that result. This is why a corrected short lag point needs an uncertainty assessment as well as a positive value. If measurement errors were temporally correlated, the correction would instead be $2[\sigma_m^2-C_\nu(\ell)]$, where $C_\nu$ is their covariance at lag $\ell$; a constant subtraction would then need revision.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p41 sha256 b34ca6facaa856c70d89c10707022b3451e9117295904d69214d8b204a9b611d -->
p41, lines 41-41; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p39; next: p43

> For a constructed Brownian movement with $D=0.010$ deg$^2$/s, adding independent position measurement errors with $2\sigma_m^2=0.0010$ deg$^2$ shifts every positive-lag MSD upward by the same amount.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p43 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p43, lines 43-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p41; next: p47

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p47 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p47, lines 47-47; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p43; next: p49

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 dfc1e9fee73efbdcf2bcb8235339349c0b844c47e23db31863cd93655235d556 -->
p49, lines 49-49; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p47; next: p53

> The offset comes from two independent position errors, one at each endpoint. Its removal changes the average MSD; it does not recover each trajectory’s unknown error. The Brownian movement is chosen only to make the constant offset visible. See [Measurement noise correction, Eqs. 18–19, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).

### The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion

Location: line 51, anchor `sec:EYE-FILTERED-MSD`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 53
Closing passage line: 156

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p53 sha256 0101d6c0734265a938f788bc99e58c63c0a11379abe76b55f0ca8fe03f35f881 -->
p53, lines 53-53; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p60

> The corrected MSD keeps increasing over the observed range, toward a second, while its logarithmic slope decreases and remains between one and two. Define that local slope by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p60 sha256 ffa29daf5431a65010c2171d6af6d8b676ba47b86589befb6cf9e5c9f6960a7e -->
p60, lines 60-60; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p53; next: p62

> Ordinary diffusion has $M=2D\ell$ and $\alpha=1$. A roughly constant velocity over a short interval gives displacement $v\ell$ and $M=\mathbb E[v^2]\ell^2$, so $\alpha=2$. Growth faster than linear is called [**superdiffusion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Here it describes a finite range of lags; it does not assert a single power law for arbitrarily long times.[^p2-msd-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p62 sha256 e33bf2f79a9c3d2cf4bdf793ee1c0471eceb4ce6775bdd396fe16f43ac37d4e0 -->
p62, lines 62-62; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p60; next: p68

> Could temporally uncorrelated motor noise acquire the observed MSD just by passing through muscles? A [**low pass filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} suppresses rapid input changes relative to slow ones. [**White noise**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here is an ideal zero mean input whose fluctuations at different times are uncorrelated. In continuous time its strength is defined by an intensity multiplying a delta function, rather than a finite variance at an individual time. A stable, decaying response to this noise produces a [**stationary process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the sense needed here: its mean is constant and its covariance depends only on time separation. Its position variance consequently does not grow with absolute time. Let $E=h*\xi$, where

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p68 sha256 c5ee64d67fb4139bb2976e02abe152ebbb6c401c04baf0b20e4e9df6433c5397 -->
p68, lines 68-68; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p62; next: p75

> The noise intensity $A$ has units input$^2$ times seconds. Expanding the convolution and using the delta function yields, for $\ell\ge0$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p75 sha256 cce01d885c41ed45bcd0e3c82740bd4087c5ba040a3f17e8aa7c755315402583 -->
p75, lines 75-75; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p68; next: p81

> For a stationary centered position, expanding the squared difference then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 8dc77a94cdb03285ca5537b9157718a263d3f99c8f7a4c0cabc3f62933e00f18 -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p75; next: p83

> If the kernel decays sufficiently rapidly, its overlap with a shifted copy tends to zero. The MSD consequently approaches $2C_E(0)$, a finite plateau. This reconstructs the reasoning of Eqs. 20–22.[^p2-white-noise]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 cfd38f1eba8c38832cd580dd38010703c95906e17fdb2bfce345f9797258724a -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p90

> For the one exponential filter $h(u)=\Theta(u)e^{-u/\tau}/\tau$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p90 sha256 567a6f8b08ffe904a9cd0be0f622edfb43ed136a86dbdb6dba94c99e7a72861a -->
p90, lines 90-90; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p92

> This simple filter has a short lag MSD proportional to $\ell$ and a long lag plateau. A double exponential filter is smoother at short lags, but still gives a plateau because its kernel also decays. Increasing $A$ raises the curve without removing its flattening. The paper's simulated private OMN noise, passed through muscle dynamics, is both too small and too flat at long lags. In Fig. 3b, using 2,000 heterogeneous OMNs, its contribution is about 1% of the observed variance near 900 ms.[^p2-msd-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p92 sha256 d99c6e36130f3060da8ce8653156df6baa9a8ebe1948ebb480525ce2177578f4 -->
p92, lines 92-92; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p90; next: p94

> Filtered white noise cannot explain continued long-lag growth. Suppose instead the input is a stored position $B(t)$ undergoing Brownian motion with $\mathbb E[(B(t+\ell)-B(t))^2]=2D\ell$. Its position variance accumulates. A low pass filter with nonzero static gain smooths the path while retaining that accumulating long time displacement variance. The following one exponential example is a teaching reconstruction, not the paper's full muscle model. It derives how smooth movement can have a slope between one and two even though the stored position undergoes ordinary diffusion.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p94 sha256 e947a3d4f4a1b23611f8aa204b2bb149785cfb02f5ccc687d1eb10b059e0d5aa -->
p94, lines 94-94; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p92; next: p100

> Let

p100, lines 100-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p94; next: p106

> $W$ is a Brownian process with unit increment variance per unit time, so $D$ has units deg$^2$/s. Define velocity $v=\dot E=(B-E)/\tau$. Taking its increment gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p106 sha256 5282ab793765fc39b4e9df56c74dc171e7d37e79ac45138ae4011e83fe52e20c -->
p106, lines 106-106; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p100; next: p114

> Velocity loses its past value over time $\tau$ and receives new random input. After its velocity transient has decayed, its solution is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p114 sha256 77982f2934e213bed74656dc75146d62d0d0dc6fd78039297d985a7132009ce4 -->
p114, lines 114-114; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p106; next: p125

> The covariance follows by multiplying the two integrals: only their shared increments contribute, with the same exponential overlap as in the white noise calculation. Integrating velocity over an interval gives position change. The double integral over its covariance reduces to

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 9e017f94ea3a08ca3f895cb3f314a9531f130a3879b2a838c87dd868655fd426 -->
p125, lines 125-125; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p114; next: p131

> At short lags, $1-e^{-\ell/\tau}=\ell/\tau-\ell^2/(2\tau^2)+\cdots$, so

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p131 sha256 d88a03ac030220b4957eb65cbe06b4f6921ffa0944c5c18e7f1f0b8eff7081b6 -->
p131, lines 131-131; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p137

> The eye keeps approximately the same velocity over that interval. At long lags,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p137 sha256 d91f634421f125a5144cad031e4f506e8cbfee428b6993a1a1b8e71edb860a17 -->
p137, lines 137-137; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p144

> which grows linearly rather than approaching a plateau. Its local slope is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p144 sha256 f29b95bd4cc0c9a4f048367ca7122106a00151b9360febdc88c252c8ef2d69c8 -->
p144, lines 144-144; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p137; next: p146

> approaching two at short lags and one at long lags. At $\ell=\tau$ it is $e-1\simeq1.718$; at $\ell=5\tau$ it is about $1.239$. Thus an intermediate slope can arise from filtering ordinary diffusion, without faster than Brownian accumulation inside the memory circuit.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p146 sha256 2acc3bd06f03088a218b49a00fd5ff2aa8d002eab278340cd813b78e86126b5b -->
p146, lines 146-146; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p144; next: p148

> The curves compare the two inputs after the same filter with one exponential response, also called a one pole filter.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p148 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p148, lines 148-148; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p146; next: p152

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p152 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p152, lines 152-152; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p148; next: p154

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p154 sha256 2894ed49380f13ff5a2d4f249bd2bc463d9cd789b6cad2610a87b57c2b77b0ca -->
p154, lines 154-154; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p152; next: p156

> The white-input short-lag slope of one belongs to this one-pole example.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p156 sha256 7a1b0ab6a5e4cd83c686d7a37e7dfdba33320bd8e5aa2c18c60e02364b3cfec1 -->
p156, lines 156-156; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p154; next: none

> The actual muscle response contains fast and slow exponential components, and the full model also contains motor neuron filtering and feedback. Those ingredients change the detailed curve. The one exponential derivation teaches the distinction relevant to Fig. 3: a decaying low pass filter of stationary white noise saturates, whereas the same filter, with nonzero static gain, retains accumulating displacement from a wandering position. A slow correlated peripheral source is a different hypothesis from the temporally uncorrelated noise rejected by this comparison.

## ch:EYE-CIRCUIT - A noisy memory circuit can produce eye drift

Source file: `content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md`

Purpose: The recordings support a shared source before the motor neurons, and the movement's timing is compatible with a stored position that wanders. Neither result identifies the circuit that supplies that command. A noisy memory circuit is a proposed explanation whose output must pass through the motor neurons and muscles before it can be compared with eye movement.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT, ch:EYE-TIMING, ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION

Sidecar sections: A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering, Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale, The figures test shared drive, diffusion, and the full circuit model, The evidence places shared drive upstream without identifying one circuit

Blocks: 0 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md::sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL -->

### A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 85

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 eacb0612bb83ad26dc3c1da2b1afeda1529552493d67197223a2d90d0c15fee9 -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> The recording analysis places much of the drive upstream of OMNs. To test whether integrator noise could supply it, the authors build a model from a noisy memory circuit through to the eye. The model adapts an earlier goldfish integrator architecture to primate tuning and variability. There are two populations, with excitation within each and inhibition between them. Their firing increases for opposite directions of gaze. This supplies the recurrent interaction needed to maintain a range of stored positions.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 2c55ee2c6b6164e165046b3628b1610bb123b45dafe2dc76be02dc1cdcf2a568 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p18

> For the right population, the model's rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p18 sha256 670a618c0d9e32cdfcced57c0ccebb39ddc8097dbe087aa1f4cefc79375840e2 -->
p18, lines 18-18; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p20

> $E_{OI}$ is the internally represented position in degrees, $\zeta_i^R$ is a tuning slope in spikes/(s deg), and $\lambda_i^0$ is a rate offset in spikes/s. Taking the maximum with zero prevents negative firing. $F$ is a feedback input in degrees, defined below. The matching left neuron has the opposite slope, $\zeta_i^L=-\zeta_i^R$. In an active neuron, a change of one degree changes its rate by $\zeta_i$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p20 sha256 0fefbab2856e1562ae4a490a06ce94073bb6cf614e00e721593ea1af74d60be4 -->
p20, lines 20-20; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p18; next: p28

> Spikes produce synaptic activity according to

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p28 sha256 af7902f51d056f623d1f9e3bfe92c3cd163f3085baba4c42d920c06b2e517fc8 -->
p28, lines 28-28; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p20; next: p30

> Here $\xi_i^R$ is a spike train generated at rate $\lambda_i^R$, $\tau_s$ is the synaptic decay time, and $\lambda_t=60$ Hz sets the rate scale in the nonlinear synaptic response. Treating spikes as counts, $X_i$ and $S_i$ are dimensionless, and the weights $\eta_i$ are in degrees. Between spikes, $S_i$ decays exponentially; one spike adds a jump of $1/[\tau_s(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)]$. Replacing spikes by their mean rate gives a drive $\lambda_i^R/(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)$ that approaches one as the rate increases. This is the model's saturating synaptic effect, rather than the purely linear synapses of the basic 2012 example.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p30 sha256 644dd42008e965323f60d3c11968b02f946c0972597764d6e0a09d713b40cf1a -->
p30, lines 30-30; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p28; next: p38

> The readout $E_{OI}$ feeds back into the rates, so these equations define a recurrent network. Persistence requires a further condition on that feedback. Set $F=0$ and replace spikes by their mean rates. Define the mean saturated drives at a proposed stored value $z$ by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p38 sha256 4e85e73974ec9b54a93f3b2d6ada8057939635f18aab8c0a679c6ecf72ab1cf3 -->
p38, lines 38-38; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p30; next: p46

> Multiply each synaptic equation by its readout weight, subtract the left contribution, and sum. Since the same $\tau_s$ applies to every unit, the readout obeys

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p46 sha256 e6a2513f8858d08feee8803fd608767f0d15c0c1779cf42a9163cb098060ee08 -->
p46, lines 46-46; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p38; next: p53

> An exact continuum of persistent readout values would require $G(z)=z$ over the represented range. For a small displacement from a fixed value, Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p53 sha256 3d4fd9a575f1c0417e726edf74b09503c18f6a1002aa184352294f17464a8fd7 -->
p53, lines 53-53; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p46; next: p55

> Thus $G'(z)=1$ is the local neutral condition for the stored coordinate. Stable directions of the remaining synaptic state are also needed for an attractor. The weights were optimized to approximate the fixed-point relation across $-50$ to $50$ degrees. Residual mismatch can produce deterministic drift, so this finite optimized circuit provides approximate persistence rather than an exactly neutral continuum. The tuning slopes and thresholds were sampled to match reported macaque relationships and variation. With spikes, perturbations along the nearly neutral direction additionally accumulate. This reduction is a teaching derivation from the paper's equations; Figure 4 separates the resulting internal diffusion from later muscle smoothing.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 bec9b956b359fac96d7d94ee37835ffd88b40ba3cfc1da3207f747ad35d26ab5 -->
p55, lines 55-55; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p53; next: p63

> The motor neurons receive this position signal:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 dc789f13c057db91a7975ce8b20dae47a49a8bfc0a45404054f3ebe109469778 -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p65

> $\lambda_i^M$ is a motor rate in spikes/s, $E_{T_i}$ is that neuron's position threshold in degrees, and $k_i$ is its position sensitivity. $\xi_i^M$ is its generated spike train. The filtered output $s_i^M$ also has units spikes/s, with decay time $\tau_{MN}$. One spike makes a jump of $1/\tau_{MN}$; at constant mean rate, the mean output is that rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p65 sha256 ddcb220d0dfb7202998c334244af8f386a828df6a8544acf98825fc373b6d8b8 -->
p65, lines 65-65; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p72

> Each motor unit's muscle and plant response is represented by a position contribution $E_i$ satisfying

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p72 sha256 3c2d953faf07527bf3bcdf292f35e5db291ea8ddb0a0835a7b880a1c398e068f -->
p72, lines 72-72; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p65; next: p74

> These are Eqs. 28–31. The second order equation uses the same roles and units as the forward fit. At constant $E_{OI}$, an active unit has mean $s_i^M=k_i(E_{OI}-E_{T_i})$, giving mean $E_i=E_{OI}$ when derivatives vanish. Averaging therefore transmits the common position while reducing independent private fluctuations. This is an effective model of combined motor output, not a claim that one motor unit moves an isolated eye of its own.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p74 sha256 136a8be33cc7785e4a5baecf0bb1832cea6b8f9f8a858e32423042a32d9d832c -->
p74, lines 74-74; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p72; next: p76

> The model used diverse thresholds and response coefficients, with motor thresholds sampled between $-45$ and $-5$ degrees, and imposed $m_i<r_i^2/(4k_i)$ to give nonoscillatory individual response kernels. Main Methods report $N=30{,}000$ integrator neurons, $N_m=1{,}000$ OMNs, and $\tau_s=20$ ms for the main full simulation. The separate private OMN comparison in Fig. 3 used 2,000 OMNs. These neuron counts belong to different simulation comparisons.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p76 sha256 8f07618e944efcd34835f24a99793429fdb45a9eb8f0aa016cb6bd2a12d40534 -->
p76, lines 76-76; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p74; next: p83

> The final loop is delayed visual feedback:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 157ca33f74a870b3a44e71a52b201d3d89c57dbdd45a3120e4fe723a47144f6f -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p76; next: p85

> Both positions are in degrees, so $A$ is dimensionless in this formulation. The main value is $A=0.015$. If gaze and its internal representation have recently moved rightward, the delayed position is to their left, making $F$ negative. Its contribution to the integrator rates then opposes continued rightward movement. The difference can serve as an estimate of recent movement; it is not an error relative to a fixed absolute target position. For a slowly varying position that the eye tracks, $E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)\simeq-\tau_d\dot E(t)$, showing the velocity-like action and sign. The approximation requires tracking and slow change over the delay.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 c748cf8e7e32992c2f33cde2c252ae4998dccb11b1ef9cadf3a0c76263e78fcd -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p89

> The effect on the ongoing MSD must be checked from the coupled model, rather than inferred from the delay alone. A short MSD lag is a separation between two samples in a running process, not the time since a new perturbation. Past measurements can already affect both samples, and $F$ also contains the immediate term $-A E_{OI}(t)$. Extended Data Fig. 3 compares the model with and without feedback: feedback changes amplitude across lags, while its larger effect on logarithmic slope occurs at longer lags. Figure 5 shows the full model. Muscle filtering supplies the short-lag smoothness, and feedback improves the detailed longer-lag fit. These are results of this model comparison, not a hard delay-based cutoff or a claim excluding visual control under other tasks.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md::sec:EYE-SCALING -->

### Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale

Location: line 87, anchor `sec:EYE-SCALING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 89
Closing passage line: 151

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p89 sha256 b75f1bebc79a5392039811391f72577a1b8fa774dffaa0695d8ce43c5d08914b -->
p89, lines 89-89; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p91

> An agreement in amplitude is informative only if we know how much freedom the model has. The paper uses regular spike trains. The [spike variability section](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-poisson) defined the interspike interval coefficient of variation (CV) as the interval standard deviation divided by its mean. A Poisson process at constant rate has CV one; the model uses about 0.22 for integrator neurons and about 0.07 for OMNs. Such firing is called [**sub-Poisson spiking**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here because its long window count variability is lower than a Poisson process at the same rate, under the interval construction used in the model.[^p2-cv]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p91 sha256 426484c85a1fa9facac2aadc3757571dcf77e291c22e7490984488a5dad39d4f -->
p91, lines 91-91; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p89; next: p93

> The construction starts with a Poisson process at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th spike. At constant rate, a retained interval is the sum of $M$ independent exponential intervals. Each initial interval has mean $1/(M\lambda)$ and variance $1/(M\lambda)^2$. The retained interval therefore has mean $1/\lambda$, variance $1/(M\lambda^2)$, and CV $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean rate while reducing variability. Its use at changing rates is part of the model; the simple interval derivation assumes a constant rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p93 sha256 1c34aa437a2473f6ff12b4d03a98c480e59c43356f8cf27507f3da19fe1cf438 -->
p93, lines 93-93; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p91; next: p101

> At constant rate, the retained intervals are independent. This is a [**renewal process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a spike process whose successive intervals are independent and identically distributed. Let their mean be $\mu=1/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sigma_I$. The time to $n$ spikes has variance $n\sigma_I^2$. For a long fixed window $L\simeq n\mu$, a fluctuation in total interval time produces the opposite fluctuation in count divided by $\mu$. Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p101 sha256 2bc134b802d4a537f31e94ff71be2e235dfbb232bd3bb07565c61e78c0d34fbf -->
p101, lines 101-101; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p93; next: p103

> This explains why the diffusion scale contains CV squared: it is the strength of the accumulated spike fluctuations relative to the Poisson case. The [diffusion calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion) and [information bound](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) show how projecting those fluctuations onto a neutral memory direction adds a factor proportional to $1/\tau_s^2$ and, for a comparable independent population, $1/N$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p103 sha256 ea92a5944f6936bf14c08b8eb5a686309ccd3a64074dcb0fbd8b0fd3d02a5a2f -->
p103, lines 103-103; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p101; next: p109

> Rate and sensitivity need more care. Write the absolute local rate slope as $a=d\lambda/dE$, in spikes/(s deg). If $N$ similar cells fire near rate $\lambda_0$, their Poisson information per unit time about position is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p109 sha256 361a2c878568912eb901ca5bab4ef2f7d63e7d0202cf670dc8960c97eae9eaf1 -->
p109, lines 109-109; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p103; next: p116

> Using the [information-diffusion relation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) as a scale, including the $\mathrm{CV}^2$ factor just derived and holding the network geometry fixed,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p116 sha256 a97e949c6ae8180c1ec415fd1e133720415886dedd04e3fdde70ef41afb02e93 -->
p116, lines 116-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p109; next: p122

> If instead sensitivity is expressed as the fractional slope $b=a/\lambda_0$, in inverse degrees, the same expression becomes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p122 sha256 1cfeeb6408d3bedd5ab3a9a7edbcbac20b11c45ae6b9d6ebc4cc9f77f751e71e -->
p122, lines 122-124; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p116; next: p126

> This second form has the dependence printed in the paper's Eq. 1, $D\propto\mathrm{CV}^2N^{-1}\lambda_0^{-1}k_0^{-2}\tau_s^{-2}$. However, its surrounding text calls $k_0$ position sensitivity without specifying fractional normalization. If $k_0$ means an absolute slope in Hz/degree, that printed expression has the wrong units for $D$; the version with $\lambda_0$ in the numerator has the required units deg$^2$/s. The guide therefore distinguishes $a$ and $b$ rather than silently treating the printed $k_0$ as unambiguous. This scaling is a comparison under fixed network geometry and noise assumptions, not an exact formula for every recurrent circuit.[^p2-scaling]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p126 sha256 42108d5c0778252cbcd35d52f426aa9e76c329e923fd7b375471446ca7dd953a -->
p126, lines 126-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p122; next: p128

> At fixed network geometry and under the noise assumptions above, the scale changes as follows:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p128 sha256 34adbe757bd5affe2fb5b060f436bc1fca3237c2ff8374e071eb38fe8056c78b -->
p128, lines 128-128; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p126; next: p129

> Doubling $N$ halves it.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p129 sha256 97eca66015ea66b16c7b4fcd50e9b39053a11b090620a6593b38a378a7f8817d -->
p129, lines 129-129; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p128; next: p130

> Doubling CV multiplies it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p130 sha256 7041a8942a6d60632e76aa235eebc13fc76689b7c5b5a105d7defaf4d65f0af1 -->
p130, lines 130-130; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p129; next: p131

> Doubling $\tau_s$ divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p131 sha256 c69f6d406939a6610f247f8d90681c726715052db1426b91bbea5c0677918062 -->
p131, lines 131-131; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p130; next: p133

> Doubling the absolute slope at fixed rate also divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p133 sha256 06f7f147b3a19159f11c1fc61d8859dc82f294ebe2307310419ae00aecd4451b -->
p133, lines 133-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p135

> Increasing rate reduces diffusion when the fractional slope is fixed, because more spikes then also supply a proportionally steeper absolute slope. Increasing rate at fixed absolute slope is a different comparison and increases the scale in the first formula. A rate change alone does not specify which comparison is intended.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p135 sha256 96cd80b8d962767580705fe9c3c707439e072e0abeb2940437a471e7a6524b04 -->
p135, lines 135-135; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p133; next: p142

> Could the measured CV itself mostly reflect drift rather than private spike variability? Supplementary Notes test this. Let $\lambda(t)=\bar\lambda+aB(t)$ during a fixation of length $T$, with the rate changes small relative to $\bar\lambda$. For a Brownian path starting at zero, the expected time averaged squared position is $DT$, and the expected squared time mean is $2DT/3$. Subtracting gives the expected within-segment position variance $DT/3=2DT/6$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p142 sha256 72378fdd398d6e247d1a7cc8ca3b5e733fe43ff9b996e8ca59dadc25f329176a -->
p142, lines 142-144; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p135; next: p151

> The second term follows from $\mathbb E[B(t)B(s)]=2D\min(t,s)$ and $T^{-2}\int_0^T\int_0^T2D\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds=2DT/3$. Expanding $1/(\bar\lambda+aB)$ to first order gives $1/\bar\lambda-aB/\bar\lambda^2$. Dividing its standard deviation by its mean then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p151 sha256 e5b6617e73dc132c58fd249a77b9dba9c1d4461bbaac83be5095e135cc171db0 -->
p151, lines 151-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p142; next: p155

> With the supplement's values $a=1/15$ spikes/(s arcmin), $2D=100$ arcmin$^2$/s, $T=0.5$ s, and $\bar\lambda=80$ Hz, this is about $0.0024$, well below the OMN CV near 0.07. These are an order of magnitude check, not a fitted diffusion constant for the whole model. Since one degree is 60 arcmin, that $2D$ equals $0.0278$ deg$^2$/s, or $D=0.0139$ deg$^2$/s.[^p2-cv-drift]

In the no-central-drive control, $\widehat\chi=-0.09\pm0.22$ is compatible with zero; that negative estimate is sampling variation. Figures 6–8 document cell classification, segment sampling, and a rank correlation check. Figure 9 shows the heterogeneous model parameters. Figure 10 separates spiking noise passed directly to the output from spiking noise that drives diffusion inside the integrator: internal stochastic spikes remain in the latter comparison. Its caption reports little direct output-noise effect beyond 50 ms.[^p2-supp-figures]

p167, lines 167-167; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p163; next: p169

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 13eb9938bf9c6236208fc52e01c9ec1269b4cf02460ab63085476a39b268d851 -->
p169, lines 169-169; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p170

> The common variance estimate supports a drive upstream of OMNs, given the calibration and noise assumptions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p170 sha256 0e5d9875c42e7d1eba8d510a5e8714c4a462cd405dbbf43cc17a69bdb9d4f8c6 -->
p170, lines 170-170; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p171

> The MSD comparison supports a command that accumulates variation over the measured interval, rather than stationary white noise passed through a decaying muscle filter.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p171 sha256 a53b626e8087382134210e2ebd356cd81cc400d4dac48406313cec70350ad76b -->
p171, lines 171-171; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p170; next: p173

p173, lines 173-173; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p171; next: p175

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p175 sha256 60d3b8c4b2e125b9ad975602b8cee5a697c525c785976bd88b6e38fae52c497b -->
p175, lines 175-175; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p173; next: p176

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p176 sha256 617ad29267837c3232f4196d91fd6ba06c2ea3c2d71dbb9ea776f3ddffac9cc6 -->
p176, lines 176-176; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p178

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 b9ed2b9f82218ee6be868a4ae150648ad5420cc8c73ab1abbca6d7ee0ecd2adf -->
p178, lines 178-178; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p180

> Noisy inputs to the integrator, or an already diffusive upstream signal, remain possible. The authors make this distinction in the Discussion and explicitly in their peer review response. Several combinations of neuron count, variability, and synaptic time constant can also fit an amplitude, so matching one curve does not uniquely recover those parameters.[^p2-localization]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p180 sha256 e90133b47eae4fd2b41bea14ed4fbcd84fab6245e8e39ca2a6b45d74f07524b8 -->
p180, lines 180-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p178; next: none

> The study's empirical scope is horizontal motion during these fixation segments in two animals. Visual tasks, feedback, and other sources of input can alter the statistics. Its contribution to the connection with the 2012 theory is more precise: a continuous memory can diffuse internally, while a mechanical output of that memory looks smooth and superdiffusive at shorter lags. To compare a memory theory with behavior, the mapping from stored value to measured movement is part of the calculation.

## ch:PLACE-FIELDS - Field counts and gaps reveal threshold and scale

Source file: `content/chapters/05-place-fields.md`

Purpose: A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?

Prerequisites: ch:FOUNDATIONS, ch:PERSISTENCE

Sidecar sections: Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields, Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction, Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Blocks: 2 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::ch:PLACE-FIELDS -->

### Chapter opening

Location: line 1, anchor `ch:PLACE-FIELDS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 3
Closing passage line: 9

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 19544e5d571507d682fd9f78dbeb35e3ca9e95cda1a21fcc7f7e39c72f6431bc -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 fd9fd365a43ffa239f2316b9104d766f576a9e7739754f0fd417dd1ba3c16e72 -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak propose that a cell's spatial input is a smooth random function. The cell fires where this function exceeds a threshold. The model predicts distributions of field lengths, areas, and volumes, rather than the location of a particular cell's next field. Its strongest tests ask whether fitting field sizes and counts also explains field shapes and holes.[^p3-question]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 94f66f9b0d8db8b88fabb359bc34b53d9b8be9d958461329ef264b77ecfed540 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p9

> We begin on a track, where a field starts at an [**upcrossing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a point where the input crosses upward through the threshold. Counting these crossings gives the average number of fields. Combining that count with the lengths of active fields and inactive gaps then separates the effect of the threshold from the distance over which the map changes. These calculations establish the parameters needed for the later predictions of sizes and shapes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 dd669fa529d2c81e07c41648d0caa39abcfbe693ad1459cfe653995e1ac6ae7f -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> Spatial randomness here describes different possible rate maps. Once a map is drawn, it is fixed while the animal moves through it. The spatial coordinate is not time, and the model does not by itself describe memory drift, spike timing, or learning.

### Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields

Location: line 11, anchor `sec:PLACE-GP`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 13
Closing passage line: 74

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 cf3931630bd66d71de8518d443fc4b71f41ade0a3e68c41d522d4c3108417994 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p15

> To predict a field's width, we need to relate activity at nearby positions. A histogram of firing rates alone loses that information: rearranging its values could turn one broad field into many tiny fields without changing the histogram.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 6bf4ed6cec6671e28df85e0d8c8761fece961e5d77b2583573a2795f97375957 -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p22

> A [**Gaussian process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a probability distribution over functions such that the values at any finite set of positions form a jointly Gaussian random vector. For positions $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, the vector $(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_n))$ therefore has a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Its mean vector and covariance matrix are obtained by evaluating two functions,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p22 sha256 eec1c95f91c43f0256bd63565aa300fed990137023b64bf8e41c77f03288f73c -->
p22, lines 22-22; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p24

> The expectation runs over possible maps, or over cells assumed to share these statistics. These two functions determine every finite joint distribution. A Gaussian process can thus produce many different curves while keeping a precise rule for their spatial dependence. A [**jointly Gaussian vector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is one for which every linear combination of its entries is Gaussian. For this family, zero covariance between two subvectors implies their independence. That extra property, which does not hold for general distributions, will simplify the crossing calculation. The values after thresholding will generally not have a Gaussian distribution.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p24 sha256 d7eef26ec39623ebdec3dffdc340f6e1f1b0ead389f12766cc2cbb9e896c627d -->
p24, lines 24-24; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p22; next: p26

> Three assumptions have different jobs:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p26 sha256 b677530192d9f2c407d66780ce867a8e11b13efc09d69bfbbbe38fb7c8494770 -->
p26, lines 26-26; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p24; next: p27

> [**Stationarity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that moving the observation window does not change the map's probability law. Here it gives a constant mean and a covariance $C(x,y)=C(y-x)$ that depends on separation. It does not say that a particular map is flat.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 78a0cae645c9dca356425d4224e74bbeac78cd7ffa93df07308fbd11ed275677 -->
p27, lines 27-27; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p26; next: p28

> [**Isotropy**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that rotating the separation does not change the covariance: in several dimensions, $C(\Delta x)$ depends only on $\|\Delta x\|$. It rules out a preferred direction in the model's statistics. Stationarity alone does not imply isotropy.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p28 sha256 8faa751c5cdd5175a3feb82bb9148730ca4eb3e45d9bf2ba13a987e12a9dbdb5 -->
p28, lines 28-28; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p30

> [**Smoothness**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} controls whether the map has well defined spatial derivatives. Crossing calculations require differentiable sample functions and nondegenerate joint distributions: the relevant vector of heights and derivatives must have an invertible covariance matrix, rather than obey an exact linear constraint. Peak calculations require second derivatives and enough regularity to control their local approximation.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p30 sha256 5a6c710f5eb1fa49372466cb9cee2871bd9af0811a97934e8b588612e815ad85 -->
p30, lines 30-30; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p28; next: p32

> A Gaussian distribution of values does not guarantee this smoothness. For example, a covariance proportional to $e^{-|x-y|/b}$ has a sharp corner at zero separation and does not supply the differentiable paths assumed below.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p32 sha256 f19aba453f15007b3477fd225c1ceac7a62a4b58c1fa0af170d4819308371576 -->
p32, lines 32-32; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p30; next: p40

> Set the mean to zero and write $\sigma_h^2=C(0)$ for input variance. A constant input mean can be absorbed into the threshold. It is useful to normalize the input,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p40 sha256 ce7eb0a4f37f7c549990ceb25320beb5eaa6985bcc1dda9d3463fb491977207b -->
p40, lines 40-40; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p32; next: p42

> Here $z$ has variance one, $q$ is a dimensionless threshold, and $a>0$ converts normalized input to firing rate, in Hz. The paper writes $\theta$ for $q$, $r$ for covariance, and $D$ for spatial dimension; this guide uses $q,C,d$ to avoid conflicts with earlier chapters. The original input threshold is $q\sigma_h$. In the paper's Equation 3 the firing rate is proportional to $[h-q\sigma_h]_+$, which is equivalent to this form after choosing the gain.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p42 sha256 9592ff2aea10761d39a66b053c3f004cb74088f339cf18ac94f3d60936b149ce -->
p42, lines 42-42; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p40; next: p48

> The [**excursion set**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} above $q$ is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p48 sha256 67c6eb073c01f241c6cd6231337a5a3a819177f30602af0c509d4c3fec7df59c -->
p48, lines 48-48; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p42; next: p50

> Each connected piece of $A_q$ is a model place field. In one dimension its size is length. In two and three dimensions it is area and volume. Two nearby peaks belong to one field if an above-threshold path joins them. Counting peaks and counting fields therefore answer different questions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 13a7443113bd7235868647853097382186a2f42f844defb231ad1430a9d7d59a -->
p50, lines 50-50; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p48; next: p57

> For a one dimensional stationary process, differentiation of covariance gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p57 sha256 2442f626a333929c7774a980d8033033c9da5bffa9887ab195db0428a595466a -->
p57, lines 57-57; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p64

> The minus sign comes from differentiating $y-x$ once with respect to each variable. The paper defines the spatial scale

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p64 sha256 766c616e0154b3bb719c9924855db5e4e8fa62cfd08baac4f9c34f39bbdc3798 -->
p64, lines 64-64; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p57; next: p66

> Thus $\sigma$ compares typical height with typical slope and has units of distance. In an isotropic field, the same definition applies along any unit direction. For the smooth Gaussian covariance $C(\Delta x)=\sigma_h^2\exp[-\|\Delta x\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$, it is exactly the parameter in that exponent. The paper uses a Gaussian covariance for its simulations. Other smooth covariances can share this local scale while having different longer distance structure.[^p3-scale]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p66 sha256 ebdd8b0016bad74b9e16ae42e4d433c8876d5071b8d33a62976ee07fd385b961 -->
p66, lines 66-66; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p64; next: p68

> A simulated Gaussian input map with covariance $C(\Delta x)=\exp[-(\Delta x/\sigma)^2/2]$ gives the firing map below when $q=0.8$ and $a=6$ Hz. Position is shown in units of $\sigma$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p68 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p68, lines 68-68; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p66; next: p72

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p72 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p72, lines 72-72; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p68; next: p74

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p74 sha256 bae2857bfda07b77ed9d4f22e7513bed66ad580cd33b2bc80ef50a0c7d4e4026 -->
p74, lines 74-74; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p72; next: p78

> Thresholding changes the distribution of values and can separate one smooth input curve into several fields. Both panels describe the same fixed map over space, so neither curve is a trajectory over time. See [Gaussian input and firing rate, STAR Methods Eqs. 2–3, p. e1](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=13).
