<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE -->

### Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction

Location: line 76, anchor `sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 78
Closing passage line: 134

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p78 sha256 26ffca6fa7d665145bde5d732371eea7a7bdad1b317e9e61fe6026147c797e0a -->
p78, lines 78-78; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p74; next: p80

> A field starts at an upcrossing, so its expected count can be found by counting upward passages through the threshold. A small interval around each crossing has a width determined by its slope; this lets us count crossings without locating every root.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p80 sha256 89750673492344d837763a92c89b3cd3f64839f3a984d2cf64b05477dd7eb0ef -->
p80, lines 80-80; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p78; next: p86

> Consider a differentiable fixed curve and a simple upward crossing $x_0$ of $q$, with slope $v=z'(x_0)>0$. Close to the crossing,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p86 sha256 694e8b08f760f19502fd22105dd04cf5c551d8854bcb6e89d52759c7627596f1 -->
p86, lines 86-86; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p80; next: p92

> The curve lies in the vertical band $|z-q|<\varepsilon$ for a spatial distance approximately $2\varepsilon/v$. Integrating $z'(x)/(2\varepsilon)$ over that interval contributes one. Restricting to positive slopes counts only upward crossings. Under the usual regularity conditions, averaging this counting identity gives the [**Kac–Rice formula**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for the mean upcrossing density,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p92 sha256 b2ca6c87c97a8ddc19b2da7ea251d7f81efcce17ed2d440c38075a5b5330f8ca -->
p92, lines 92-92; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p86; next: p94

> The density $p_{z,z'}(q,v)$ describes how often height is near $q$ and slope near $v$. Multiplication by $v$ compensates for the shorter spatial window of a steep crossing. Multiplying the probability of being above threshold by a typical slope would miss this weighting.[^p3-crossings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p94 sha256 30afaa7d71d1c5c40139ed8744ebc9512d14702cffbd326bfdd18012244a2666 -->
p94, lines 94-94; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p92; next: p96

> A fixed smooth curve shows how to count a field already active at the left edge and why the crossing integral weights each slope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p96 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p96, lines 96-96; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p94; next: p100

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p100 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p100, lines 100-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p96; next: p102

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p102 sha256 04bdc367a22c8716b8aab8fa75de3b51d243dc6ec03720e6789b1433a55ff7e3 -->
p102, lines 102-102; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p100; next: p104

> The count includes every connected active interval intersecting the track. Discarding clipped boundary fields would change this convention.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p104 sha256 8c29e2b994c75073980c3f8ad32714b819c26ccc7b35bd53d2de20a9b6433dc4 -->
p104, lines 104-104; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p102; next: p110

> We can evaluate the integral because height and slope are independent here. Stationarity gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p110 sha256 84fa962b3b57a4ad77ef7daa267fe687fa4c1e5f87b1c643090fb3b1ab3d7c5a -->
p110, lines 110-110; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p104; next: p124

> Height and slope are jointly Gaussian; zero covariance therefore makes them independent. Their distributions are $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ and $z'\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^{-2})$. Consequently,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p124 sha256 55ce3b5cdfc08d42f8cd193bc859364fc8b1da4765231b569c8820e7d09e5377 -->
p124, lines 124-124; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p110; next: p126

> In the integral, use $u=\sigma^2v^2/2$, so $v\,dv=du/\sigma^2$. This result is exact for the stated smooth stationary Gaussian model. A high threshold is not needed. Its units are inverse distance: increasing $\sigma$ stretches the map and reduces crossings per metre.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p126 sha256 a3178d05daf5a8383331b6a0a5b1c5e79a3b9e27e140138effb0735de2e92ce6 -->
p126, lines 126-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p124; next: p134

> On a finite track $[0,L]$, each upcrossing starts a field, but a field may already be active at the left endpoint. If $N_L$ counts all components intersecting the track, then, under regularity conditions that exclude tangencies and give nonzero slopes at crossings,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p134 sha256 7736661f9fb1708487ba89d75f45e334b953e0f0ba02c2c2a3f68d5fa0f24dad -->
p134, lines 134-134; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p126; next: p138

> where $Q(q)=1-\Phi(q)$ and $\Phi$ is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The last term is the endpoint correction. Removing incomplete boundary fields changes the counting convention, so the same correction cannot then be used unchanged. This identity also gives the paper's exact one dimensional mean Euler characteristic, Equation 8.[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-INFERENCE -->

### Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Location: line 136, anchor `sec:PLACE-INFERENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 138
Closing passage line: 181

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p138 sha256 5d7d5dc39403425b5fd9abe772ce85e311e8b051d71dda011ea792a94bb785ee -->
p138, lines 138-138; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p134; next: p140

> A low field count can result from a high threshold or a slowly varying map. Field lengths help separate those possibilities.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p140 sha256 3ba9275df1f7ac4137a1a3e303c3f773156791a58bfb30831959120f36dbfd87 -->
p140, lines 140-140; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p138; next: p151

> At a uniformly chosen position, the probability of activity is $Q(q)$. Hence a track of length $L$ has expected occupied length $LQ(q)$. On a long stationary track, the average occupied length per field is the occupied length per unit distance divided by the number of fields per unit distance. The corresponding gap calculation uses the inactive fraction $\Phi(q)$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p151 sha256 6fc750fb0ae48d8804aa2e0a57ba3d7dfaf42a38e0f59cb9564ec1a8f7ab25fd -->
p151, lines 151-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p140; next: p153

> These are stationary per-field and per-gap means, ignoring clipping at track boundaries. They are not an assertion that the expectation of a finite sample's ratio equals the ratio of its expectations. Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p153 sha256 302cfe3ed35e922f0fd2c191f88efb35fc98f0654a85332d71b8cd317ac46e33 -->
p153, lines 153-153; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p151; next: p160

> Adding and dividing removes one unknown at a time:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p160 sha256 735730f52e5c6f810adc36cacc71e4c2fa20715b18de9638770a73cd88db0e2e -->
p160, lines 160-160; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p153; next: p168

> Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p168 sha256 a4f2ea777c7db9fea0029a5b14dc58a73452ea71201e76fc031fbcc2c66d9ab7 -->
p168, lines 168-168; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p160; next: p170

> For example, $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m, the fitted parameters reported for the 200 m bat tunnel, give $Q(q)=0.1357$, $\nu_+=0.03621$ per metre, $\mu_s=3.746$ m, and $\mu_g=23.868$ m in the continuous model. Putting these two means into the inversion recovers $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m. The expected number of intersecting fields on a 200 m track is $7.2425+0.1357=7.3782$, including the left endpoint term. These are calculations from the fitted model, not fresh estimates from raw recordings.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p170 sha256 5603218cf6641f79a36df6dd40cf50b7ff3a2209c4a3cdd48bcf092341d01a6c -->
p170, lines 170-170; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p168; next: p172

> The means do not imply a full gap or count distribution. At high thresholds, distant rare excursions can approach independent events if correlations decay sufficiently with distance. In that regime, field locations approach a Poisson process, counts in a long interval are approximately Poisson, and gaps are approximately exponential. Smoothness and stationarity alone do not ensure this independence. A periodic Gaussian process, for example, retains long distance dependence. Upward and downward crossings that enclose the same small field remain strongly related even when fields far apart are almost independent.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p172 sha256 d8329140074a8cfa78d595d1fb497bf52c7bb51a0b4851af51cb62d749a92fe0 -->
p172, lines 172-172; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p170; next: p181

> Experimental selection also matters. If cells with no fields are excluded, a Poisson model with unconditioned mean $\lambda$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p181 sha256 3889a03387b2f0ea6253d52ac9eddfcfc693e052e1672ed237823cf0423ca7d5 -->
p181, lines 181-181; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p172; next: none

> The mean follows because the omitted $n=0$ term contributes zero to $\sum n\Pr(N=n)=\lambda$, while conditioning divides all remaining probabilities by $1-e^{-\lambda}$. For $\lambda=1$, $36.8\%$ of cells are excluded and the observed mean is $1.582$, not one. Using $1.582$ as the unconditioned mean would infer too many fields. The paper applies this correction to its two dimensional count fit, Equation 5.[^p3-selection]

## ch:PLACE-SIZES - High peaks explain size laws across dimensions

Source file: `content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md`

Purpose: Knowing the average field length does not tell us how often very small or very large fields occur. Why should the same spatial model give one size distribution on a track, another in an arena, and a third in a room? The next step is to find the shape of a typical field when only unusually high input peaks rise above the threshold.

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS

Sidecar sections: Near a high peak, the input is nearly parabolic, Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension, Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume, Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Blocks: 2 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::ch:PLACE-SIZES -->

### Chapter opening

Location: line 1, anchor `ch:PLACE-SIZES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 3
Closing passage line: 7

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 2cb38e68c07bd54fd5714855b5dd37966287a741bae3cf63fd8ce2ad4deada4a -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> Knowing the average field length does not tell us how often very small or very large fields occur. Why should the same spatial model give one size distribution on a track, another in an arena, and a third in a room? The next step is to find the shape of a typical field when only unusually high input peaks rise above the threshold.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 55a3986e13e973e4e095736d1e56d450921ccd292b12bede609aeef7ca9e6e68 -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> Near such a peak, the smooth input is approximately a downward parabola. Its height above the threshold sets a radius, and converting that radius into length, area or volume gives the different size laws. Cuts through a map test its spatial structure, and slopes at field edges provide a separate check on the predicted field widths. The size laws use a high threshold approximation; the boundary-slope law has a separate exact derivation under the smooth stationary Gaussian assumptions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 26a1fe7914be17a1e3c5a4d6f987ea769b07b29a63e572b3b3b67ef972df4555 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> We keep the earlier notation: $z(x)$ is the spatial input normalized to variance one, $q$ is its threshold, $\sigma$ is its local distance scale, and $a$ converts positive excess input into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. The firing rule remains $F(x)=a[z(x)-q]_+$, where $[u]_+=\max(u,0)$.

> The [**Hessian**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the matrix $H$ of second spatial derivatives; its eigenvalues describe curvature along the principal directions. Isotropy and the definition of $\sigma$ imply

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 c8fb27ef5a829c9fdf7d0b0566b33f765c33eaa10ee7f51ed1bd1071f0d292f0 -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p22; next: p35

> Here $\delta_{ij}$ is one for equal indices and zero otherwise. For jointly Gaussian variables, conditioning $Y$ on a scalar $Z$ of variance one gives $\mathbb E[Y\mid Z=b]=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)b$ when both means vanish. One way to see this is to write $Y=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)Z+\epsilon$; the remainder has zero covariance with $Z$ and is therefore independent of it. Applying this to each Hessian entry gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 47d4b1cf84b128232a6a68f53843a3ec5ff5371866328fc9cd3b8a9d22498674 -->
p35, lines 35-35; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p37

> The conditional fluctuations around this mean have a variance that does not grow with $b$. For a high peak, the negative mean curvature is of order $q$, so its relative fluctuations become small. Conditioning the gradient to zero does not change this Gaussian conditional mean, because the gradient has zero covariance with both height and Hessian in a stationary isotropic field. Selecting an actual maximum also weights curvatures, but its leading high peak shape is the same.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 0041a3d93b0436aee52ecdbbc68f4786f15bd183b547921f61c99d063d378053 -->

> The remaining question is how $\epsilon$ is distributed when we select one high field at a time. In one dimension, the number of high maxima is asymptotically the number of upcrossings, proportional to $e^{-q^2/2}$. Therefore the fraction of peaks above $q$ that also exceed $q+\epsilon$ is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p56 sha256 9d42766e0fc8603f0a98d7761de1a9ef6a3caafc35dc5d516e261062df577e81 -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 f23475eeebfb9763ea088d5906bced5a4c281b387a47f4917baad046ea7dc5e8 -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p56; next: p65

> Thus the scaled excess height approaches an exponential variable of mean one. In several dimensions, high peak counts have the additional leading factor $q^{d-1}$. Its ratio at $q+u/q$ and $q$ tends to one, so the same excess-height limit remains. The higher dimensional count factor will also follow from the [Euler characteristic calculation](ch-place-tests.html#sec-place-topology). A rigorous conclusion requires control of the remainder and of components containing multiple peaks, beyond this local argument.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p65 sha256 2deb6f24b242404c3ab3dfa2f2642f337252e20239c3140667a1b04d3b4953b6 -->

p75, lines 75-75; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p73; next: p76

> The length is a scaled square root of an exponential variable, giving a [**Rayleigh distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p76 sha256 48eb0ee7a3f3ac15279acc3dcd205fe4856c033e991e84f3b20d87ceb93613be -->
p76, lines 76-76; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p75; next: p78

> The peak firing rate is $a\epsilon$, so at fixed high $q$ it is proportional to $s^2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p78 sha256 ad81166a7f9900050bfe0abadf3efbc49772f1507374348f00b30c7a4efadaf6 -->
p78, lines 78-78; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p76; next: p80

> The exponent two concerns rate versus linear size. In three dimensions a suitable linear size is $s^{1/3}$ when $s$ denotes volume.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p80 sha256 acdee6f644c343b31dd07894bc92b9af954e704fe99f475ff3b9568012cc8f2e -->
p80, lines 80-80; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p78; next: p84

> At moderate thresholds the omitted shape fluctuations matter. Figure 3C reports a rate versus length exponent of $1.42$ in the 200 m bat data and $1.46$ in finite threshold model simulations. Figure 3D reports $1.75$ versus $1.71$ using cube root of volume in 3D. These are compatible with a model that approaches exponent two only as the threshold becomes large; they are not measurements of the limiting exponent. Figure 5A–C illustrates the progression toward simpler, usually single peak fields as $q$ increases. Figure 4C tests this trend in the shorter bat tunnel.[^p3-shapes]

For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW -->

### Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension

Location: line 82, anchor `sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 84
Closing passage line: 169

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p84 sha256 1901fc4e63d3a5b50382c5e7752d17da5da6af0c255516e359814c81b92aeae6 -->
p84, lines 84-84; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p80; next: p86

> A round region's radius and its volume have different units and different distributions. This geometric conversion explains why the model predicts different histograms in a track, an arena, and a room.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p86 sha256 34446abce2eebaafa505521f0e0a1b7526ecbcb89dd8abc0e18fee576cfd9566 -->
p86, lines 86-86; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p84; next: p88

> Let $v_d=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)$ be the volume of a unit ball in $d$ dimensions. For $d=1,2,3$, it is $2,\pi,4\pi/3$. The symbol $\Gamma$ is the [**gamma function**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, defined for $t>0$ by $\Gamma(t)=\int_0^\infty u^{t-1}e^{-u}\,du$. It extends factorials: $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p88 sha256 2622397a97e4137834c9aecff9dee82065045328ad11e47745542a699a321117 -->
p88, lines 88-88; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p86; next: p97

> Using the radius from the preceding section, the field size is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 a27858dfe6bc00b5646aaedf4ca12865baf30d61a2c1db5ab3646c520bc7b61d -->
p97, lines 97-97; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p88; next: p99

> The same local radius gives a length, an area, or a volume depending on the dimension of the region.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p99 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p99, lines 99-99; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p103

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p103 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p103, lines 103-103; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p99; next: p105

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 e27b505efde0a2a69f8ba698465e8c43b25637897fc635212abd60e6fdc01c80 -->
p105, lines 105-105; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p103; next: p107

> The changing power comes from geometry. The round shape and exponential excess belong to the high-threshold limit, so they do not make every measured place field spherical.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p107 sha256 d7015fd40a4d70397a639649766ed3dab80232ac55cec5c395bb28e2c7e89e0c -->
p107, lines 107-107; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p116

> Since the density of $U$ is $e^{-u}$ for $u>0$, changing variables gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p116 sha256 a21700950f13cea61955cf68feb5958b2567b3e5be5df2609be8d48ac0ec2752 -->
p116, lines 116-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p107; next: p118

> This is the paper's Equation 1. The expression for $\beta$ above is its leading high threshold geometric scale, not an exact finite threshold identity. A finite threshold simulation can share the approximate shape while having a different fitted scale.[^p3-size-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p118 sha256 70e635652302213a2cf731e3084b884490fbb2f91b7de49476aeffc988347e81 -->
p118, lines 118-118; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p116; next: p127

> The mean and its corresponding scale are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p127 sha256 bd78903b899f2ba416d0a9fa0e50e6848ecdffcdf5af8bfb1f67d84cbd4075bb -->
p127, lines 127-127; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p118; next: p137

> Normalization needs no special integral: $\int_0^\infty p_d(s)\,ds=\int_0^\infty e^{-u}\,du=1$. The same substitution supplies every positive moment,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p137 sha256 e0cefd2bef21d8c57c06ba03819d1f74f6d0f41e3ad03b4ed921afc4a741b404 -->
p137, lines 137-137; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p127; next: p139

> Size has units of distance to the power $d$, while $\beta$ has units of inverse distance squared in every dimension. This makes $\beta s^{2/d}$ dimensionless, and makes $p_d(s)$ a density per unit size. These checks catch mistakes in changing variables.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p139 sha256 5eb4bde29d56abc33274fa983b0769051b44b928b70a23d40313217e78de35aa -->
p139, lines 139-139; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p137; next: p141

> Writing dimensionless size as $w=\beta^{d/2}s$ removes the size scale. The dimensional size laws then differ in shape, while the transformed variable $U=\beta s^{2/d}=w^{2/d}$ has the same exponential law.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p141 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p141, lines 141-141; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p139; next: p145

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p145 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p145, lines 145-145; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p141; next: p147

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 f3deb54ce8a9e5943cb5082baae607c47048ff2203832e1ac17c5f91a6a0cb9f -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p149

> Removing units does not erase the difference between length, area, and volume distributions. The nonlinear transformation to U removes that shape difference; its shared exponential law is the assumption used in the change of variables. See [Field-size laws, Eq. 1, p. 1111](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=3).

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 9960784fd6b96a12fbf5b8855619bf1a26dae079bce219c3b96e0c64ec075ad2 -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p153

> <div class="table-responsive">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p153 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p153, lines 153-153; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p155

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p155 sha256 b0cac387c1042983a3962b519a03aca42a4d5f9b880a93c1b0417150203c90f8 -->
p155, lines 155-155; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p153; next: p157

> A [**Weibull distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} has survival probability $\exp[-(s/b)^k]$ for size $s>0$, scale $b>0$, and shape $k>0$. Here $k=2/d$ and $b=\beta^{-d/2}$. In 3D the diverging density does not mean a positive probability of exactly zero volume; the probability of $0<s<\delta$ still tends to zero as $\delta\to0$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 1198b5a52fcf5236f4556770b0651c726dee0edcbce5726be8f473d67ea222db -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p155; next: p159

> For a concrete comparison, set $\sigma=1$ m and $q=5$ in this limiting calculation. The mean size is $0.5013$ m in 1D, $0.2513$ m$^2$ in 2D, and $0.1260$ m$^3$ in 3D. These values describe the same local distance scale and threshold, rather than fields with the same numerical size in different units. More generally, the geometry and moment formula simplify to $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p159 sha256 aa3180403722280994445aa61ea622d099572f5c9047af1eba1ce1233166be10 -->
p159, lines 159-159; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p163

> This approximation has a measurable error even for a smooth Gaussian process. At $q=1.1,\sigma=2.4$ m, its 1D mean is $5.469$ m, compared with the [exact stationary crossing result](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-inference) $3.746$ m, an overestimate of $46.0\%$. At $q=5$ the error is $3.73\%$. This is why the paper compares data to finite threshold simulations as well as to limiting curves.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p163 sha256 ff864c1077a2589498fd3bf05187ff3d7e4d9a4009d33a0cfb023d7fb36e0d27 -->
p163, lines 163-163; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p159; next: p165

> The printed supplement appears to contain three algebraic errors in this calculation:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 916af41df8e7a07fcadee25de0f885c71ee63808c3a11e063878ac3469d10f1b -->
p165, lines 165-165; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p163; next: p166

> Equation S18 prints multiplication by mean size where the preceding moment formula requires division.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p166 sha256 cbdcb17a297de8911bad66e90999d69ea4f98394709189fef43cfa0b609e7be5 -->
p166, lines 166-166; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p167

> Equation S23 omits a factor $\sqrt2$ when solving the parabola in S22 for full width.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p167 sha256 6e39c1527f2b69e4322110f808587d200d1f28916006e58fe58cf3424b5662ba -->
p167, lines 167-167; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p166; next: p169

> In S25 the finite-height quadratic exponent should be negative.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 afeb2d1b47a2c8122dee33aba308b6231e92c7619d3dfe22ca505f0e2cae403c -->
p169, lines 169-169; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p175

> The derivation above retains the main paper's distributional form and obtains the constants from the displayed parabola, the moment integral, and the independent crossing calculation. These corrections concern the printed derivation; they do not establish that the simulation code or empirical fits used the same mistakes.[^p3-corrections]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLICES -->

### Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume

Location: line 173, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLICES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 175
Closing passage line: 188

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p175 sha256 f7a4d1df563695a6f456b6601656f1ad5be30d37c443f44aea6f10e8f88cc427 -->
p175, lines 175-175; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p177

> A model can fit field volumes and still place activity incorrectly within those volumes. Slicing the map tests that internal spatial structure.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p177 sha256 7fcf2dfddf9b5536a5156f479958372adf7f807b8bf2cec9a245d5fda588393c -->
p177, lines 177-177; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p179

> Restrict a Gaussian field to a straight line $x=x_0+tv$. At any chosen values $t_1,\ldots,t_n$, its values are a subset of the original jointly Gaussian values. They therefore remain jointly Gaussian. Along the line the covariance is $C((t-t')v)$; for a unit direction in an isotropic field it has the same local length scale $\sigma$. The same argument applies to a flat plane. First slicing and then thresholding gives the same active portions as thresholding and then slicing.[^p3-slices-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p179 sha256 92a306ecd5eec60105b9f9987ed8da5e18e423dd44d473ac381bb4733ac32c70 -->
p179, lines 179-179; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p177; next: p181

> Thus a line through a 2D or 3D map has the one dimensional excursion prediction, and a plane through a 3D map has the two dimensional prediction. The field being counted is now a connected active interval or patch in that slice. One 3D component can be missed, cut into multiple pieces, or intersect several slices.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p181 sha256 3715b83572476e5d4162bb74795b4c995d300529eeb05ffbd97764edfe1f3d55 -->
p181, lines 181-181; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p179; next: p183

> The sampling rule matters. Choosing one original field uniformly and cutting through its centre is different from choosing lines uniformly through space. Large fields are more likely to be hit by a spatially sampled line, and most hits pass away from their centres. The Gaussian restriction argument describes excursions along the sampled line. It does not justify assigning an unchanged field-counted radius distribution to all observed chords. In the high peak picture, an isotropic $d$ dimensional field has a radius of order $\sigma/q$; converting this same radius into $d$ dimensional size and then cutting it requires both hit probability and cut position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p183 sha256 dcbb4960853c0a7ca89e7d923d8f592935f10cada772e0a1560dac9b68666ed7 -->
p183, lines 183-183; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p181; next: p185

> Figure 2 separates the measurements by spatial dimension:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p185 sha256 1f9b91b1e977a0b284df50042941b94319781a39609cb7614e1d497d44f6d3c4 -->
p185, lines 185-185; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p183; next: p186

> Panels A–B illustrate 2D thresholding. C measures lengths along slices, D measures original field areas, and E counts fields per observed cell.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p186 sha256 fe81365d724dec1c56d7d5d27277e05053bbf816b7b1fd4e5a0d6ee7b616b8ec -->
p186, lines 186-186; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p185; next: p188

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLOPES -->

### Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Location: line 190, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLOPES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 192
Closing passage line: 216

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p192 sha256 e344c1bebf0db137fc241848261e512767ee4d9a2a050e602e340fdef310b0a0 -->
p192, lines 192-192; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p194

> The derivative at an arbitrary position is Gaussian. A field boundary is a selected position, however, and selecting it changes the derivative distribution.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p194 sha256 fb4d93baeb2d07a0468341c3f611f160bb689ff9747952eaaad5691a739b2303 -->
p194, lines 194-194; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p192; next: p202

> Return to the [crossing integral](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-kac-rice) and retain only slopes between $v$ and $v+dv$. Their contribution is proportional to $v p_{z'}(v)dv$. Dividing by the total contribution of positive slopes gives the slope density at a typical upcrossing:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p202 sha256 22f7985306579c6dae07453b788a543b49a4a30b7302abc69192d261fc39dc1c -->
p202, lines 202-202; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p194; next: p206

> The height density cancels because height and slope are independent. This Rayleigh law is therefore independent of $q$ under the smooth stationary Gaussian model, without a high threshold approximation. Absolute slopes at downward boundaries have the same law. The rate slope is $av$, so its scale also depends on the firing rate gain. This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p206 sha256 65eb0b476fa28ef5ac949160ef950c255718a10344956fe5e81ac9a5dd55fc0a -->
p206, lines 206-206; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p202; next: p212

> There is also an independent check on the high threshold width constant. Starting at an upcrossing with slope $v$, the local excursion is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p212 sha256 8a1dc3346431d47dcdafec9e28737546824f0fb1d2eb9dfcbcabc9771538b7a2 -->
p212, lines 212-212; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p206; next: p216

> Its second root is $s=2\sigma^2v/q$. Changing variables in the exact crossing slope law gives $p(s)=2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$ with $\beta=q^2/(8\sigma^2)$, the same constant as the peak calculation for $v_1=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p216 sha256 594efec82ac884dd9d09a6353fa67727a6b437fd2fe0866144d93a9a8f14f3c9 -->
p216, lines 216-216; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p212; next: none

> Figure 3A identifies which width, peak, and slopes are measured. B compares local peak counts, C–D compare rate and size, and E compares boundary slopes. The paper measures boundary slopes by a finite difference between samples 0.1 m apart. This approximates a derivative and has its own spatial resolution. It is not a direct observation of the underlying synaptic input.


Claim-bearing content: none

- Claim c2, line 196: "$$ p_{\uparrow}(v) =\frac{v p_{z'}(v)}{\int_0^\infty w p_{z'}(w)\,dw} =\sigma^2v\exp[-\sigma^2v^2/2],\qquad v>0. $$" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c3, line 202: "This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source]" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]

## ch:PLACE-TESTS - Holes and measurements test the spatial explanation

Source file: `content/chapters/05c-place-tests.md`

Purpose: Two maps can have the same field sizes and counts while arranging their active regions differently. One may contain rings or tunnels that the other lacks. Can the Gaussian spatial model account for these differences, and how much would that agreement tell us about the circuit that produced the maps?

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS, ch:PLACE-SIZES

Sidecar sections: Holes distinguish maps with the same field count, What was fitted and what was tested, Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps

Blocks: 1 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05c-place-tests.md::ch:PLACE-TESTS -->

### Chapter opening

Location: line 1, anchor `ch:PLACE-TESTS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 3
Closing passage line: 7

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 5c7249f482cb9944fdc543fc113eab535dc0f2c7f32ab960a08447841f993715 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> Two maps can have the same field sizes and counts while arranging their active regions differently. One may contain rings or tunnels that the other lacks. Can the Gaussian spatial model account for these differences, and how much would that agreement tell us about the circuit that produced the maps?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 2e3309c33081b2eaac4e0945b5a0ed403df1d85e2d55ca39616ed4ab8c71551c -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> We first count connected regions and holes together, which gives a prediction across activity thresholds. The boundary of the observed environment matters for this calculation, so we keep its contributions. We then examine which measured quantities set the model's parameters and which provide further tests. Finally, a sum of many randomly weighted inputs shows one way a circuit could produce Gaussian spatial input, while making clear which assumptions and circuit measurements that explanation still needs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 5bb1f6982a8d2f312b9d5b8748fe3ca08dcf18f2c5923f2091b0926d6e7be8f2 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> The map is still fixed over space. As before, $q$ is the normalized activity threshold, $\sigma$ is the local spatial scale, and $a$ converts normalized input above threshold into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$; it is separate from the diffusion coefficient $D$ used in the memory chapters.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05c-place-tests.md::sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY -->

### Holes distinguish maps with the same field count

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 117

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 dcc3b4afcb6573eacb3352443986df40586f55ef693a5fc13d5bea28cd0d3ade -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> Two maps can have the same field count and total active area but different holes. As the activity threshold rises, active regions shrink and can split or disappear. The numbers of components and holes can change.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 247c26246edb0dfb23d3d0f7017e467d983d2d8d4832064cef52958313d16b84 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p15

> The [**Euler characteristic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\chi$, combines these counts differently in each dimension:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 6493b64c127b0011b545370586936c17f7222669fa417de7cf73ec92c070655d -->
p15, lines 15-15; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p16

> In 1D it equals the number of connected active intervals.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p16 sha256 e80754190f1e0c1656f81f4735a2b540833f465734dc911c120a55ba6d010d3c -->
p16, lines 16-16; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p17

> In 2D it is the number of connected components minus the number of independent holes. Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$; one connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 f6c9a4588ef99b3239033d5ecc56a4ee4f69ab2872575aad354694ebdd772e19 -->
p17, lines 17-17; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p16; next: p19

> In 3D it is components minus independent tunnels plus enclosed cavities. A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 3a928ce08d9cdfda9961ab4aea0aca53eddd35f1fafd170b33b0358f0a56c74a -->
p19, lines 19-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p21

> These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 f0d912c242f1c3298afc120785c32067c289e55b31ec386020154b4944f6df02 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p23

> Thresholding one fixed smooth map separates the number of connected fields from the number of holes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p27

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p27, lines 27-27; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p29

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 be79eb69f6109d899f86e8bac03bee74dbf19681aa49cf26f8bc258eee48b58a -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p31

> A negative chi means that holes outnumber components in this 2D example. It does not mean a negative number of fields. When holes are absent, chi equals the component count.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p31 sha256 82810fa88fe25895abd23e8454d8ba307a5acc613dd966e1209d0d9530c88451 -->
p31, lines 31-31; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p33

> The expected Euler characteristic has an exact Gaussian formula even where identifying it with field count would be wrong. We already derived the 1D case. To understand the higher dimensional factors, we use a mathematical result: for a smooth function on a compact domain whose [**critical point**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s have zero gradient and invertible Hessians, the Euler characteristic of a level set region can be computed as a signed count of critical points, including boundary contributions. This is the relevant conclusion of [**Morse theory**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. We do not prove that topological theorem here. The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms; the general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p33 sha256 9d413cbb462a8805e6f97d941c734d4c3a1a0e7be354b3687908cb6fe05e8c05 -->
p33, lines 33-33; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p31; next: p41

> First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$. A critical point has gradient zero. The multidimensional Kac–Rice rule counts these zeros with the absolute Hessian determinant; the Morse signs replace that absolute determinant by $\det(-H)$. The resulting signed density above level $q$ is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p41 sha256 a54adb9dc66647dc11a47e1a99103408b5b1516b6a0959b97f26244ec1647232 -->
p41, lines 41-41; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p33; next: p43

> where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$. The determinant measures how spatial volume near a gradient zero converts to gradient volume, just as the slope did for a scalar crossing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p43 sha256 710dd87df9b25e8475fe88f2037ff2191ed664f4e4b000fe059c857b143b6732 -->
p43, lines 43-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p41; next: p51

> The resulting signed densities are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p51 sha256 a7b5eaf9118abeb9aa49bb2332a925d2d12b0f8fadb10cec749495df1f5cc4a1 -->
p51, lines 51-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p43; next: p55

> These are signed densities per length, area, and volume, not three formulas for positive field counts.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 a43eb99f7072b08aea13f4bddee0b295009d95289a7d528c6b5cc3653298ebc9 -->
p55, lines 55-55; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p51; next: p63

> Here are the Gaussian algebra steps behind the determinant average. Write the conditional Hessian as $H=-b\lambda I+G$, where $G$ has zero mean. Differentiating an isotropic covariance four times gives, for distinct indices $i,k$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 611e89c52f18e27cbd5d4f4abe1873b2e7174ca8a18394cf1b65f67d44e707a4 -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p69

> The constant $\kappa$ describes a fourth derivative of covariance. Conditioning on height subtracts $\lambda^2$ from the covariance of the two diagonal entries, but not from the off-diagonal variance. Hence each pair contributes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 6eeec516802772ad652d966ac77d65a1e4ea637da1f35a5fe49eb8ff995f112f -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p71

> For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$. For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$. For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$; the three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish. The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$. The unknown fourth derivative $\kappa$ has canceled.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 ccb3f22095106d1560d4d4abd636f04939cba9b69067374fb410037ad38bb37d -->
p71, lines 71-71; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p81

> Integrating the three polynomials gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 1932d4d5337ab09c2c8efb724627a22c5f4745d76b0c90944dba46389d9ef93b -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p71; next: p85

> For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint. Combining the determinant, gradient density, and integral gives the signed densities above.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 2c20f8c2c868fed491d30dc311931c8e5599abc6b9fa53e204f7213a6e3121d8 -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p98

> Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained. On a $j$ dimensional face, derivatives along the face give the same calculation as above. Each of its $d-j$ gradient components perpendicular to the face and directed out of the box must have the appropriate sign to contribute to the boundary Morse count. Symmetry and independence give a factor $2^{-(d-j)}$. There are $2^{d-j}\binom dj$ such faces, each of size $L^j$. Their combined coefficient is therefore $\binom djL^j$. Corners supply $Q(q)$ in total. With $r=L/\sigma$, the formulas become

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p98 sha256 326eb6657b7e2ac143f23713a457ffa350de85acbf14c87f48af0b7096e91aab -->
p98, lines 98-98; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p100

> These reproduce the paper's Equations 8–10 for interval, square, and cube geometry. They are not the formulas for a periodic box. For a rectangle or rectangular room, replace $2L$ or $3L$ by the sum of side lengths, $3L^2$ by the sum of pairwise side products, and $L^3$ by volume. A general domain requires its own geometric boundary factors. Applying only the volume term to a small room would discard real boundary contributions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p100 sha256 693938cc06386ff5efcb5591233f271e8708277170c58071134b6cf32d2cb7d3 -->
p100, lines 100-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p98; next: p102

> As $q\to-\infty$, the whole box becomes active and all three expectations tend to one.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p103 sha256 33bb49f8aa1b54e50dfb140b4f9fea863a54fb803b3a695f47796c8010f47d59 -->
p103, lines 103-103; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p102; next: p105

> As $q\to+\infty$, they tend to zero.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 1e79b0b04b033c2327f0604cd81864e15fabe4744aa9bf378e6246102c0aef42 -->
p105, lines 105-105; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p103; next: p107

> At intermediate thresholds the higher dimensional signed densities can be negative because tunnels or holes outnumber components. For example a cube with $L/\sigma=10$ has expected $\chi=-20.06$ at $q=0$. A negative expected Euler characteristic is meaningful; a negative expected field count would not be.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p107 sha256 b19a80efc6ef3cebb84771df7a93a897523e424ec20f0935d3698bb0248e429e -->
p107, lines 107-107; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p115

> At a sufficiently high threshold, almost every component is a small solid blob with one peak and no holes. Then $\chi\simeq N$. For a large box, retaining the leading volume term and the leading power of $q$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p115 sha256 5eee88f6ac607536212e2dbb3e3b9df22f068e713fb3e6dcf608eb26d6fd3b62 -->
p115, lines 115-115; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p107; next: p117

> where $V$ is environment length, area, or volume. Dividing $VQ(q)$ by this count and using $Q(q)\simeq\phi(q)/q$ recovers $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$, independently checking the size-law mean. The exact EC formula and this approximate component count have different ranges of validity.[^p3-higher-count]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p117 sha256 378192a1076eb211507796aa3a77b10ceff0b27946bf47939d54b587c90b9f7b -->
p117, lines 117-117; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p115; next: p121

> Read Figure 3F–H as whole curves, rather than single field counts. Increasing the applied firing rate cutoff by $t\ge0$ changes the normalized level to $q+t/a$. It can reveal separation between peaks or remove components. The measured maps only test levels at or above their original effective cutoff; the rest of an analytic curve is a prediction. The gain was matched using mean maximum firing rate before the EC comparison, so the horizontal conversion from Hz to normalized level also uses fitted information. Two dimensional holes and three dimensional tunnels help explain the nonmonotonic curves.[^p3-ec-source]

> The experiments supply estimated firing rate maps, not the hidden Gaussian input. To assess a prediction, we must therefore track both which summaries set its parameters and which processing decisions change the map's geometry.

The inspected article and supplement do not clearly give the final included cell count for every analysis, particularly the long-tunnel and 3D reanalyses; counts from an original recording study should therefore not be silently substituted for those final samples.[^p3-processing]

> The STAR Methods call the virtual-track animals rats in one sentence; Figure 1F and the main results identify them as mice. Several STAR figure cross-references also appear displaced. The table follows the main figure captions and the analyses described in full, rather than those isolated labels.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p133 sha256 b55ee79d7aea3b1a19361ea9e5846e43fac2d02da16b50f97c8e77eda4c0dc2c -->
p133, lines 133-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p135

> Figure 1C distinguishes a limiting analytical Rayleigh curve from the finite threshold simulation histogram. Its parameters were constrained by both mean length and mean gap, so the full length histogram is an additional check. Figure 1D does the same for gaps. E tests skew and excess kurtosis of log lengths. For $Y=\log s$, write $m_k=\mathbb E[(Y-\mathbb EY)^k]$; then [**skew**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_3/m_2^{3/2}$ and [**excess kurtosis**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_4/m_2^2-3$. A [**lognormal distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} of sizes makes $Y$ Gaussian and both population quantities zero. Finite sample estimates still fluctuate, which is why the figure compares simulated sampling distributions.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p135 sha256 e0ff8f69b6e4e7b7cc04aa3cd2ba415f0e2f0172ac511dbd6a34b2ad24437307 -->
p135, lines 135-135; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p133; next: p137

> For the long-tunnel data, the authors compared the Gaussian excursion model with a fitted lognormal using likelihoods and criteria that penalize fitted parameter count. Those comparisons favored the Gaussian model. These are in-sample model comparisons. They are not reported held-out forecasts. Model histogram error bars come from $10^4$ simulations matched to the number of recorded cells; they do not quantify every source of animal-level or measurement uncertainty.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 a301c59952a1b6dbddb9f300f4508459b73c64f13e0c3a357bf091227148a5f0 -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p149

> The supplement adds useful checks. Figure S1 matches the rat maze's 15 cm minimum length cutoff in simulations and removes unusually large empirical fields; changing that cutoff changes log-length moments. Figure S5 shows sensitivity of the higher-moment comparison across small denoising thresholds, while the rate versus width slope becomes stable above about 1 Hz. Figure S2 compares the spherical-field alternative and measures boundary curvature. Locally, [**mean curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} averages bending in two principal directions and [**Gaussian curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} multiplies them: a spherical patch bends the same way in both directions, while a saddle bends in opposite directions. These are extra shape measurements; they are not needed to derive the field-size law. Surface representations and finite differences affect their estimates.[^p3-supp-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 f3ab73148350b61d76a33097435dc578a9f1ca338b1d4d18ec526535397d61ca -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p151

> Figure 4A–B summarizes fitted parameter changes across a small set of environments. The correlation length increases with environment size while the threshold generally decreases. Increasing $\sigma$ reduces crossings per metre; decreasing $q$ increases them. Both parameters also affect mean length, so these effects must be considered together. Figure S3 reports count scaling exponents below one and hence a weak decline in count per unit environment size. These cross-experiment trends do not establish a biological scaling law. In particular, the 3D table's reported $\sigma=4.0$ has an unclear distance-unit conversion relative to the spatial binning, so it should not be read as a verified value in metres.[^p3-trends]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p151 sha256 b55c4804307932ad51ca9f7cae0073f9ba356aad17cd6b5853035339596dd39c -->
p151, lines 151-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p155

> The model also allows differences between cells. Figure S4 draws a positive threshold separately for each cell and keeps that threshold for two otherwise independent maps representing opposite flight directions. Low-threshold cells tend to have more and larger fields in both maps; this shared cell property creates correlations between flight directions even when field locations differ between directions. Two distribution parameters were chosen to match the two empirical correlations, so those correlations are fitted features of this extension. Its continued agreement with length and gap distributions is the additional comparison. This extension should not be described as a two-parameter model shared by all cells.[^p3-threshold-variation]


Claim-bearing content: table

### Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps

p155, lines 155-155; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p151; next: p157

> The statistical model becomes biologically useful if it suggests how a circuit could make the maps. A random weighted sum of many inputs is one possible mechanism, and the supplement makes its assumptions explicit.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 d4754d9c681c5a9275cb92565a055d40d544fb8d2dfc5be32d26301a27b68779 -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p155; next: p163

> Let $u_i(x)$ be the fixed spatial tuning of input neuron $i$, and let independent weights $W_i$ have zero mean and variance $v_W$. The profiles $u_i$ may have nonzero spatial means. The expectation below is over weights: their zero means make the summed input $h(x)$ have mean zero. The postsynaptic input is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p163 sha256 bd69187085e59bca3bf71c3f09586e171956015e2201fa49b5c5586309f76996 -->
p163, lines 163-163; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p165

> At a finite list of positions this is a sum of independent random vectors, one per presynaptic cell. Under a multivariate central limit condition, with a suitable normalization and no single input dominating, the sum approaches a Gaussian vector as the number of contributing inputs grows. Applying this at every finite list supplies the Gaussian-process description. The theorem needs many contributing inputs at the positions in question; a large anatomical input count alone does not show that this condition holds.[^p3-wiring-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 d2a002d6434381ff1dc22fa84811a52d1e42a1ec251760afaadf1cda16ded894 -->

p175, lines 175-175; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p181

p184, lines 184-184; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p183; next: p185

p185, lines 185-185; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p184; next: p187

> Random weights supply the Gaussian limit under their independence and size conditions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p187 sha256 470067af25c56c95daa214e4ff83a553f08feb992ff3976f87f6c3840992861e -->
p187, lines 187-187; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p185; next: p189

> For example, take $u_0(s)=R_0e^{-s^2/(2b^2)}$ in one dimension. Completing the square gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p196 sha256 98b28bbeb7835ea3e4c956ee74d5e7ca58b3c878e8246b85f50b22fdc3763114 -->
p196, lines 196-196; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p189; next: p198

> The covariance's Gaussian standard deviation is $\sqrt2 b$, fixing the width convention used in the comparison.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p198 sha256 e835b9977f7aed2f3e810f609834c0207090ed4589a1870f20eacd948c6210d8 -->
p198, lines 198-198; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p196; next: p200

> Agreement with the Gaussian field predictions supports this random-summation account as a possible explanation. It does not uniquely recover synaptic weights from rate maps. Correlated inputs, a structured component added to many random contributions, plasticity, or recurrent processing could also produce approximately Gaussian spatial input under some conditions. The paper measures rate maps, rather than the relevant weights or subthreshold input directly. Its analyzed environments were familiar and fairly uniform; stationarity may fail near landmarks, boundaries, rewards, or in changing tasks.[^p3-discussion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 3938564b29656cee9c9faf1eee89fa9cba0fa180eef7a7079df6e737ca1fd7e5 -->
p200, lines 200-200; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p198; next: none

> The result to carry into the synthesis is therefore specific: thresholding a smooth spatial Gaussian field links field arrangement, shape, and topology through a small set of parameters and gives several successful cross-checks. Turning that statistical agreement into a claim about circuit mechanism requires additional measurements or perturbations.

## ch:SYNTHESIS - Spatial maps and memory dynamics need separate tests

Source file: `content/chapters/06-synthesis.md`

Purpose: A model can explain the shape of a neural code without explaining how that code is maintained. It can also explain fluctuations inside a circuit without uniquely locating the source of a measured behavior. Comparing the three papers requires keeping each claim with the mathematical or experimental test that supports it.

Prerequisites: ch:MEMORY-READOUT, ch:EYE-CIRCUIT, ch:PLACE-TESTS

Sidecar sections: The three papers constrain maps, dynamics, and movement, How a static map can carry a moving estimate, Example: opposite rate changes from the same position error, Each model needs a test matched to its claim, Related studies change assumptions and leave circuit choices open, A joint test would compare tuning maps with population fluctuations

Blocks: 1 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-QUESTIONS -->

### The three papers constrain maps, dynamics, and movement

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-QUESTIONS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 25

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 0b80f24a27e581a9c2f5e86fe66ccc8e2ca7aaf40b5983a4a102100b07388aff -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> For a circuit representing position, three questions arise:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 a9a4d7fa3d020076c9f6f82d4de4ee3f54b1dd07ac0833e9a89b6b2bc55a7da3 -->
p11, lines 11-11; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p12

> **Spatial map:** What activity pattern corresponds to each position? The place-field model addresses this question.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p12 sha256 1ab2eef073fbfc193d9f2724f85d531095153a9491777e56d38a4f564305f00f -->
p12, lines 12-12; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p13

> **Memory motion:** How does the represented position evolve when there is no corrective input? The persistent-activity model addresses this question.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 3c8bc1157fe708f7af2b8d416c696bb1cb5b548a00af076d59a022f20713d336 -->
p13, lines 13-13; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p12; next: p15

> **Observed movement:** How does a downstream physical system transform that changing signal into an observable movement? The eye-drift model combines a candidate account of memory motion with a detailed account of this transformation.[^s-three]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 5f0d743f2d1f49e3a96956d00be8a1bf6f9a70134b740a7f87c145add2891d5f -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p17

> The objects differ even when the word position appears in all three. In 2012, $\theta(t)$ locates a network state on its attractor. In 2022, the internal eye-position command and actual eye angle are separate quantities linked through motor neurons and mechanics. In 2025, $x$ locates an animal in an environment, and $h_i(x)$ is a spatial input function assigned to a cell. The Gaussian process does not update a remembered $x$ through time.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 3bce5cfb23a3680a57742cebd05e7ee506a21490d771b6e569e19be03db625af -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p19

> The [guide map](ch-persistence.html#fig-foundations-paper-map) gives a visual comparison of these three questions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 9960784fd6b96a12fbf5b8855619bf1a26dae079bce219c3b96e0c64ec075ad2 -->
p19, lines 19-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p23

> <div class="table-responsive">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p25

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p25 sha256 37ffdd708ba8474c6b1445eee3bb7c1ed842b4c97da9f56fa84d04086d56644c -->
p25, lines 25-25; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p29

> The 2012 and 2022 connection is direct: neural noise can move a maintained eye-position command, which then drives muscles. The 2022 result provides evidence for a substantial shared source before the final motor neurons and shows that integrator diffusion is sufficient within the constructed model. It does not measure the circuit's Fisher information or test equality in the 2012 bound. The place-field model describes spatially irregular tuning curves. To connect those curves to memory dynamics, we must also specify how the represented position moves.


Claim-bearing content: table

### How a static map can carry a moving estimate

Location: line 27, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-JOINT-CALCULATION`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 29
Closing passage line: 81

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 bcaeedd7bff71c036d24e1ce99ad14c710194c7262c4c3303f02611ee8e03a4a -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p25; next: p35

> Suppose a network maintains a one-dimensional estimate $x(t)$ and cell $i$ has a fixed expected-rate function $f_i(x)$. Its time-dependent expected rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 52960562ed8a66c6287ff261c2b2e31a70e029b0a57df378e7696dd4eae09fec -->
p35, lines 35-35; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p37

> Here $f_i$ describes spatial tuning and $x(t)$ describes dynamics. A source of time variation has entered even though $f_i$ is fixed. Actual spike counts add another layer of variability around the expected rate. This construction is a guide calculation connecting the papers' objects, not a model tested jointly by the three studies.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 26ec0a2ba2dd9d70b8a18d86e0a17bc2c5aa95313651452ae8db960664f8c046 -->
p37, lines 37-37; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p44

> Consider a short interval of duration $t$ starting at $x_0$. Assume that the estimate's local displacement $\delta x=x(t)-x_0$ has mean zero and variance $2Dt$, and that it is small enough to linearize each rate curve. Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p44 sha256 84605c14ed46c29ad7def1fcb1f9ae4615ec24f004367b7e54f6a6a7710a93e4 -->
p44, lines 44-44; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p37; next: p52

> To leading order, the change is $\delta R_i=f_i'(x_0)\delta x$. Multiplying the centered changes for neurons $i$ and $j$ and averaging yields

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p52 sha256 999290bb61c28849dadda74211bbbac06315dce9f77c1e44aab3636d71d726ea -->
p52, lines 52-52; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p44; next: p56

> This covariance concerns expected-rate changes caused by the common represented displacement, conditional on the starting position. It is not a formula for every covariance in a spiking recurrent network. It neglects higher-order changes in tuning and additional private or shared fluctuations. Near a threshold or over a large spatial displacement, the linear approximation must be revisited.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-COVARIANCE-EXAMPLE -->

### Example: opposite rate changes from the same position error

Location: line 54, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-COVARIANCE-EXAMPLE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 56
Closing passage line: 81

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p56 sha256 f96cb6dfc04493e98b98a5974085326ceac39a611e1a2675b4a9ba715be224f9 -->
p56, lines 56-56; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p52; next: p69

> Let the local slopes of two rate curves be $f_1'=2$ Hz/m and $f_2'=-1$ Hz/m. Suppose $D=0.01$ m²/s and the interval is 0.5 s. Then the variance of the common position displacement is $2Dt=0.01$ m². The contributions to rate variance and covariance are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 48433ea57f29595c3bc120cb3f5db4a6e290fcf35a3e630b5974341a5d7cd7fd -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p56; next: p71

> With only this one source, the two rate changes have correlation $-1$: one rises whenever the other falls. Adding independent private noise lowers the magnitude. The negative covariance here comes from opposite tuning slopes under a common position change. It does not establish an inhibitory connection between the cells.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 c2f56feecf56a500fc37c79c93127ee82bf3c2739fd8a45c784c7317b5fd16b8 -->
p71, lines 71-71; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p73

> For the same slopes, set both expected rates to 10 Hz at the starting position. The upper plot shows the fixed rate curves; the lower plot shows their changes under a common position displacement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p73 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p73, lines 73-73; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p71; next: p77

p79, lines 79-79; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p77; next: p81

> Both changes are set by the same position displacement, so they occupy one direction in the two-rate plane. Independent private fluctuations would spread them away from that line.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 30aa1177ec0bcac3fde988ac1bca3e585aeb9dbd9fea7ac3943ec583d79b3eac -->

- Claim c6, line 69: "With only this one source, the two rate changes have correlation $-1$: one rises whenever the other falls." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c7, line 75: "Caption: A common displacement of $+0.1$ m increases rate 1 by $0.2$ Hz and decreases rate 2 by $0.1$ Hz. The changes lie on one line. The 10 Hz baseline is chosen only to display positive rates...." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-EVIDENCE -->

### Each model needs a test matched to its claim

Location: line 83, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-EVIDENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 85
Closing passage line: 85

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 bf26aa5ad95f5aa3bc6f807fedf818b62a3e2c4b2b3ae9dfd59c6b4ec7d43255 -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p87

> The shared-position calculation predicts how rates would change together. Establishing that connection requires measurements of those changes as well as the spatial maps. Each paper tests a different part of the proposed connection:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p87 sha256 08c5b1584f195f6b0470fd74fe62b5e7a6b74a88c5e5b461e8df1efc516588ad -->
p87, lines 87-87; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p89

> **Place-field statistics:** A model that matches one distribution may have enough adjustable parameters to do so for several different reasons. The place-field paper gains support by fitting arrangement parameters and then comparing additional features, including slices and shape statistics. The separate firing-rate multiplier used in some comparisons should remain visible when counting how many quantities were predicted without further fitting. A successful high-threshold formula is also a statement about its limiting assumptions; finite-threshold simulations and the original recordings provide different kinds of checks.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p89 sha256 74808e1ca267c6539fddf1e8c68152214e385a4e8faeb35d526f50f2c20490e9 -->
p89, lines 89-89; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p87; next: p91

> **Eye drift:** The eye paper calibrates a movement-to-firing relationship, inverts it, and compares the resulting single-neuron displacement predictions with measured displacement. Shared covariance then carries information that independent private fluctuations cannot supply under the decomposition's assumptions. The additional MSD comparison tests the time structure: increasing stationary motor-noise amplitude cannot by itself reproduce the accumulating upstream signal. Calibration validity, source correlations, two animals, and horizontal movement remain part of the result's scope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p91 sha256 bea6060b31430bed48ff70aa2cf55916410d6839d4bc94fd293b81aa8efab917 -->
p91, lines 91-91; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p89; next: p95

> **Memory stability:** The 2012 inequality is a mathematical constraint rather than an experimental fit. Its derivation specifies what noise and dynamics it covers. An experimental comparison would need estimates of diffusion, the relevant time constants, and the appropriate instantaneous information rate, all under compatible conditions. Agreement with $1/N$ scaling alone would not test the entire inequality, since several mechanisms can share that scaling.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-RELATED -->

### Related studies change assumptions and leave circuit choices open

Location: line 93, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-RELATED`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 95
Closing passage line: 95

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p95 sha256 2fa4f402fb3c9140a5e2737da457f19171d7f49686a14d7e5e2060ddbf538125 -->
p95, lines 95-95; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p91; next: p97

> Related studies test the assumptions and ask questions the three papers leave open:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 fb7c417261e9f39abe25e9c54a0513aab6392fbaa5858b11b366f012db803015 -->
p97, lines 97-97; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p95; next: p99

> **Integrating movement:** The eye-position account begins with Seung's line-attractor model. Aksay and colleagues then used recordings and perturbations in goldfish to constrain integrator circuitry. These establish the dynamical problem and experimental architecture behind the 2022 model. Neither supplies the macaque displacement-variance estimate. Burak and Fiete's earlier grid-cell study examines [**path integration**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, updating a position estimate from movement. In its model, velocity drives recurrent population activity, and errors accumulate in the position estimate. Its accuracy ranges belong to the simulated networks and parameter choices, rather than all biological grid circuits.[^s-attractors]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p99 sha256 246e640967b354dad7572f93f73a8a92bf512c987cd27a6e3debb2ef10642eab -->
p99, lines 99-99; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p101

> **Storage capacity and changing synapses:** Noise-driven storage also connects to memory capacity. Koyluoglu and colleagues ask how noise limits recall as the delay and number of stored items increase. Their comparison with human data shows why direct storage of several independent values need not capture observed behavior; transformations before and after storage alter the performance prediction. Seeholzer and colleagues examine how short-term changes in synaptic strength affect diffusion and systematic drift. These papers change the storage assumptions rather than invalidate the 2012 calculation inside its original model.[^s-extensions]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p101 sha256 35ecf69af1e00f263011e694a4578723a886e6b09ba2380aba18ba9c4469a0de -->
p101, lines 101-101; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p99; next: p103

> **Recordings from larger environments:** For place fields, Eliav and colleagues' long-tunnel recordings and Harland and colleagues' large-arena recordings supply the multiple-field, multiple-scale observations. Yartsev and Ulanovsky supply the three-dimensional bat recordings. The 2025 paper reanalyzes those existing data, so its agreement across dimensions is not three newly collected experiments under one protocol. The later study by Maimon and colleagues directly compares CA3 and CA1 in large tunnels: predominantly single fields in CA3 contrast with multiple fields in CA1, while field sizes are similar. That distinction supports treating the CA1 statistics and the upstream spatial circuit as related but separately constrained parts of the explanation.[^s-place-data]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p103 sha256 b7a1cc065d5c2ad942ab5ecd1ca0052ab0188751d98e397ce4eb9886c4bc39e2 -->
p103, lines 103-103; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p101; next: p105

> **Connectivity and learning:** Gaussian spatial statistics do not identify a unique pattern of connections. Druckmann and colleagues report structured synaptic connectivity between hippocampal regions. Bittner and colleagues show how electrical events in [**dendrite**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s, the branching input structures of neurons, can induce changes in synaptic strength and form CA1 place fields. Structure and learning can therefore be present even when a random-function model describes many aggregate field statistics. The model's success leaves open which circuit mechanism produces these maps. Input, connectivity, learning, or perturbation measurements could distinguish the possibilities.[^s-wiring]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 0ff5e5f6c91d8070a3687305852c8017a4c42373f6fa5489195fa9f6d61ba483 -->
p105, lines 105-105; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p103; next: p109

> **Benefits for perception:** The source and use of eye motion are different questions. Nghiem and colleagues combine human eye tracking with a model of how eye movement affects visual signals to explain why an intermediate amount of fixational motion can help fine visual discrimination. A movement that partly originates from internal noise can still have useful sensory consequences, and feedback can regulate its statistics. Benefit for perception does not uniquely identify the neural generator.[^s-acuity]


Claim-bearing content: none

### A joint test would compare tuning maps with population fluctuations

Location: line 107, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-NEXT`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 109
Closing passage line: 116

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p109 sha256 62880184ed300c88426f75fcc0ae7954a09243eb6436dfac51c3ecb63185029a -->
p109, lines 109-109; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p111

> A joint test needs tuning maps and fluctuations from the same population. A possible sequence is:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p111 sha256 dcf643870dff7a8c4d647743e1e653d482f30cc97b7b4b18c08365b0c2bfe28e -->
p111, lines 111-111; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p109; next: p112

> Estimate the tuning maps and their uncertainty.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 8abed503bea87e5f7ca0fa95cefe7acf4ad69e22af38581e2701b568c035b4ca -->
p112, lines 112-112; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p111; next: p113

> Measure fluctuations when external signals provide little correction of the represented position. Test whether their covariance aligns with the changes implied by the tuning maps.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p113 sha256 ba5c6c1493d5d94d8f7d9010d5ef5bdd1e6bb4a4c6f7a38da8f695dcf749f75f -->
p113, lines 113-113; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p112; next: p114

> Fit diffusion only over a range where the local approximation and measurement corrections are justified.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p114 sha256 7fb58465a9c8a72b3907619e2e542823ad8d388d2c66ccdd3badfce559784de2 -->
p114, lines 114-114; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p113; next: p116

> **If comparing behavior,** include the physical or neural filters between the representation and the measurement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p116 sha256 c537125369470a7f35ab03d1d20ae2e553c43afbd9711807edfadd01ab38db2e -->
p116, lines 116-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p114; next: none

> This would ask whether one system satisfies both spatial-statistics and dynamical-stability constraints. A static Gaussian-process fit alone cannot supply the diffusion coefficient. A diffusion fit alone cannot establish the distribution of fields. A joint success would be more restrictive, while a mismatch would point to a particular assumption to change: spatial input statistics, recurrent maintenance, the observation filter, or their coupling. This is proposed follow-up work, not a replication claimed by this guide.
